<meta>This page begins the actual laboratory instructions for creating Dr. Francis Anthony's "Potable Gold." It focuses on the first stage of the process: the calcination (burning) of tin.</meta>

# Dr. Francis Anthony’s Recipe <note>original: "Receit"</note>
## *showing the way to make his most Excellent Medicine called Potable Gold.* <note>original: "Aurum Potabile." Latin for "drinkable gold," a legendary alchemical substance believed to be a universal cure.</note>

---

Take block tin <note>original: "Block-tinn." High-quality, pure tin cast into blocks, which was the starting material for this preparation.</note> and burn it in an iron pan. Make the pan red-hot before you put the tin in, keeping a continual fire under it and stirring it constantly until it looks like ashes. Some of it will look red. It will take at least a day, or half a day, to burn. It must be stirred with a small iron coal-rake <note>original: "Cole-rake." A tool used for scraping or moving materials in a furnace or hearth.</note> with a handle two feet long.

G. H. M. <note>These initials likely refer to a specific chemist or assistant who practiced this method.</note> made an iron pan a foot and a half long and a foot wide, with edges <note>original: "Brims"</note> two inches deep. He built an oven in a chimney with bars of iron at the bottom where he placed the pan, with a space underneath to build a fire. In this way, it will burn more quickly (namely, in half a day); the smoke will not damage it.

<vocab>Dr. Francis Anthony, Potable Gold, Block tin, Iron Pan, Coal-rake</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the initial step in creating "Potable Gold," which surprisingly begins with the lengthy process of burning block tin in an iron pan until it is reduced to ashes.</summary>
<keywords>Francis Anthony, alchemy, Potable Gold, calcination, tin, laboratory equipment, chemistry, 17th-century medicine</keywords>

--- Page 90 ---
<meta>This page continues the instructions for the preparation of "Potable Gold," following the initial burning (calcination) of block tin into ashes.</meta>

# Dr. Francis Anthony’s Recipe

->*Keep these ashes in a glass, tightly covered.*<-

Take 4 ounces <note>original symbol: ℥ (ounce)</note> of these ashes and 3 pints of the strongest red wine vinegar. Put them into a glass vessel shaped like a urinal <note>original: "Urinal." In early chemistry, this was a specific type of round-bottomed glass flask with a long neck used for digestion and distillation.</note>. After putting the ashes in first, seal <note>original: "lute." To seal a vessel with clay, wax, or cement to make it airtight.</note> the vessel and let it stand in a hot water bath <note>original: "Balneum." Latin for bath; here referring to a "balneum mariae" or bain-marie, used for gentle, even heating.</note> for 10 days. When that time is up, take it out and let it cool. Let it stand for 2 or 3 whole days so that the sediment <note>original: "Feces"</note> may sink to the bottom. The glass must be shaken 6 or 7 times every day.

Let the clear liquid run out, unfiltered, by using 2 or 3 woolen threads <note>This refers to "filtration by ascent," a method where liquid travels up and along threads via capillary action to separate it from sediment.</note> into a glass basin. Distill it in a glass still until all the liquid has been drawn off. Pour this distilled liquid <note>original: "distilled Water." In this context, it refers to the distilled vinegar essence, not plain water.</note> onto 4 ounces of fresh ashes.

Onto the old ashes (from which the first liquid was filtered), also pour a quart of strong red wine vinegar. Seal the glass as before and put it into the water bath; let it stand there to digest for 10 days. Filter this and distill it as described above. Thirdly, pour one pint of the same vinegar onto those ashes and put it in the water bath for 10 days. Filter it and distill it as before. After this third infusion <note>The process of soaking the ashes to extract their chemical properties.</note>, throw away the ashes.

<meta>catchword: Distill</meta>

<summary>This page details the extraction process where tin ashes are repeatedly soaked in red wine vinegar and distilled to capture their chemical essence.</summary>
<keywords>Francis Anthony, distillation, vinegar, laboratory equipment, extraction, chemistry, tin ashes, infusion</keywords>

--- Page 91 ---
<meta>This page continues the specific laboratory instructions for preparing the "solvent" and the gold needed for Dr. Anthony’s "Potable Gold" remedy.</meta>

# Potable Gold

Distill all the separate infusions until the liquid is completely distilled off.

Take this distilled liquid <note>original: "Water." Here referring to the distilled vinegar essence used as a solvent.</note> as often as it is distilled and pour it onto new ashes, maintaining the same weight and sequence. Repeat these infusions, filterings, and distillations seven times.

Through this, you will obtain the *solvent* <note>original: "Menstruum." In alchemy and early chemistry, a menstruum is a solvent used to dissolve solids.</note> you seek.

You must take care that the vinegar is made from red wine and is very strong; otherwise, your solvent will not meet your expectations.

The Bishop paid Dr. *Anthony* 30 shillings for a quart of this solvent.

Take an ounce of pure refined gold (which costs 3 pounds, 13 shillings, and 4 pence). Cast it into an ingot <note>original: "Wedge"</note> and file it into fine dust with a fine file. Put this ounce of filed gold into a calcining pot <note>A heat-resistant crucible used for high-temperature heating.</note> and add enough white salt to nearly fill the pot. Set it among charcoals where it may stay continuously hot for 4 hours. (If it is too hot, the salt will melt.) When the 4 hours are finished, take it out and let it cool. Then place it on a painter's stone and grind it very fine with a muller <note>A heavy stone tool used for grinding substances on a flat slab.</note>. Then put it back into the pot and calcine <note>To heat a substance to a high temperature to change its physical or chemical state, often to make it more brittle or purified.</note> it again, and grind it again. Repeat this 4 or 5 times; if it looks red and blue when you take it out, it is perfectly prepared.

<meta>The catchword on the bottom of the original page is "After," indicating the next step in the process.</meta>

<summary>This page describes the final purification of the vinegar-based solvent and the labor-intensive process of preparing gold dust through repeated heating (calcination) and grinding with salt.</summary>
<keywords>Potable Gold, Dr. Francis Anthony, alchemy, distillation, solvent, gold calcination, chemistry, laboratory equipment</keywords>

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>This page continues the instructions for processing gold through calcination and washing to produce a "white calx" as part of the preparation for Dr. Anthony's "Potable Gold."</meta>

# Dr. Francis Anthony’s Laboratory Instructions

After this calcination and grinding, place it into a *glass* basin. Fill the basin with scalding hot water and stir it for a good while until the heavy part has fully settled to the bottom. Then, pour away that water and add fresh hot water. Stir it and let it settle as before. Repeat this process until the water has no taste of salt once it has settled; this will take two or three days.

Out of this ounce of gold, barely more than 16 or 17 grains <note>A grain is a traditional unit of weight, approximately 65 milligrams. There are 480 grains in a troy ounce.</note> will be converted into a fine white calx <note>original: "Calx." In alchemy and early chemistry, this refers to a fine powder produced by roasting or chemically treating a metal.</note>. To separate this from the rest of the gold, leave a little of the last fresh water in the basin and stir it well together. The calx will float to the top; gently pour it away from the gold into another basin. If all the white calx does not come out, add a little more *water*, stir it again, and pour it into the basin with the rest of the calx. Then, let it settle and pour away almost all the water. Evaporate the remaining liquid over heat until it is thoroughly dry, then store it in a *glass* vessel.

Next, take the gold that has not yet become calx and add salt to it as described before. Calcine and grind it four more times, then wash it and separate the calx as you did previously. Continue to calcine and wash the remaining gold in this manner until it has all been turned into calx.

<summary>This page describes the meticulous process of washing salt from calcined gold and separating the resulting "white calx" through sedimentation and evaporation. The procedure must be repeated multiple times until the entire ounce of gold is converted for use in Dr. Anthony's medicinal "potable gold."</summary>
<keywords>calcination, calx, gold, chemistry, alchemy, Francis Anthony, laboratory procedure, sedimentation, evaporation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the instructions for Dr. Francis Anthony’s "Potable Gold," moving from the creation of the white gold powder (calx) to the extraction of its "tincture" using a solvent (menstruum) and subsequent distillation.</meta>

# Potable Gold <note>original: "Aurum-Potabile." A legendary alchemical medicine consisting of gold dissolved in a liquid, believed to have universal healing properties.</note>

Take one ounce of this *calx* <note>original: "Calx." The fine powder produced from the gold in the previous steps.</note> and place it into a glass vessel shaped like a urinal <note>In this context, an "urinal" refers to a specific shape of laboratory glass vessel with a rounded body and a long neck, similar to those used for medical samples.</note> with a capacity of about one pint. Add half a pint of the *menstruum* <note>original: "Menstru-um." A solvent used in alchemy to extract the essence or "tincture" of a substance.</note> to it. Place this glass in a hot *balneum* <note>original: "Balneum." A water bath (similar to a double boiler) used to provide gentle, even heat.</note> for six days, having been tightly sealed with *lute* <note>original: "luted." Lute was a clay or resin paste used to seal laboratory apparatus airtight.</note>, and shake it frequently each day. 

When the six days are over, let it sit for two or three days. Then, very gently pour off the clear liquid so as not to disturb the *feces* <note>original: "Feces." The solid residue or sediment remaining at the bottom after extraction.</note>. Add fresh *menstruum* to this residue, though not quite as much as the first time. Repeat this a third time, using even less solvent than the second. Then, take the dry residue, which is the *calx*, and save it in case any *tincture* <note>The medicinal essence or color extracted from the gold.</note> remains within it.

Pour these colored liquids into a glass *still* <note>A distillation apparatus.</note> and distill them in a water bath, using a very gentle fire at first, until all the clear liquid has run out and what remains is as thick as honey. Then, remove it and let it cool. Next, place the glass into an earthenware pot and pack ashes around the glass inside the pot <note>This "ash bath" provides a more intense but still regulated heat than a water bath.</note>. Secure the pot firmly into a small furnace and build a fire beneath it, so that the glass stays very warm until the residue is black and very dry. 

You can hold a candle behind the glass *still* to see when the material has puffed up into clumps and dried. Then, remove your fire and let the glass become very cold before taking out the "black earth" <note>The charred or concentrated residue remaining after high-heat distillation.</note>. Once this black earth has been removed,

<summary>This page outlines the extraction of the gold's "tincture" using a solvent and heat, followed by a distillation process that reduces the liquid to a thick substance and finally a dry "black earth."</summary>
<keywords>Aurum Potabile, calx, menstruum, balneum, distillation, tincture, black earth, Dr. Francis Anthony</keywords>

->Dr. Francis Anthony’s<-

Put it into a glass basin and grind it into a powder using the bottom of another round glass. Then, place it into a glass vessel shaped like a urinal <note>original: "Urinal-like Glaſs." In early chemistry, this referred to a round-bottomed flask with a long neck, shaped like the vessels used by physicians to examine medical samples.</note> with a capacity of about one pint. To that, add a little more than half a pint of the spirit of wine <note>original: "Spirit of Wine." A term for high-proof distilled alcohol, similar to modern ethanol.</note>. Place this glass in a cold place until the liquid turns red, which will take about ten days. Shake it frequently every day, until the final three days before you pour it out. 

Then, gently pour away the clear liquid and place that clear portion into a glass still (or another glass vessel until you have more). Add more spirit of wine to the residue <term>feces</term> <note>original: "Feces." The solid dregs or sediment remaining at the bottom of the vessel after the liquid is poured off.</note> and process it as before. If that liquid is also strongly colored, add spirit of wine <note>original: "*Spiritus Vini.*"</note> a third time, just like the first. Combine all these colored liquids together and distill them until the residue (called the tincture <term>tincture</term>) is as thick as a syrup.

Take one ounce of this tincture <term>tincture</term> and add it to a pint of Canary Sack <note>original: "Canary Sack." A fortified sweet white wine from the Canary Islands, very popular in the 16th and 17th centuries.</note>. Once the liquid is clear—which will take about a day and a half—you may drink it.

### *The Preparation of the Vinegar to make the Solvent.* <note>original: "*Menstruum*." An alchemical term for a solvent used to dissolve solids or extract their essences.</note>

For the necessary glassware, obtain three or four glass stills that can hold one or two gallons each. You will also need a water bath <term>balneum</term> <note>original: "*Balneum*." A container of water used to provide gentle, even heat to the glass vessels placed inside it.</note> two and a half feet square to hold many glasses. Obtain about six gallons of the strongest red wine vinegar (vinegar made from Clar... <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword "ret," likely continuing "Claret."</note>

<meta>This page continues the specific instructions for creating the solvent needed for Dr. Francis Anthony’s "Potable Gold." # *Potable Gold*

...claret or *White Wine* are too weak) <note>original: "ret." Likely "Claret," a dry red wine from Bordeaux.</note> made of red *Wine*, Sack, or Muscadine <note>Sack and Muscadine are types of strong, fortified sweet wines.</note>. Set as many stills going at a time as your *water bath* <note>original: *Balneum.* A "balneum Mariae" or Bain-marie, used to heat substances gently and evenly.</note> will hold. Take a pint of the liquid that runs out first and put it aside, as it is too weak and not suitable for this use. Then, distill out all the rest until the still is dry. Wash the still with a little of the phlegm <term>phlegm</term> <note>original: "flegm." In early chemistry, this refers to the watery, tasteless, and less potent part of a distilled liquid.</note> from the first running, and then wipe it dry.

Then, put in the liquid that was distilled and repeat the process as before, discarding the first pint. Do this five times. Thus, from one gallon of wine, you shall obtain 3 pints of the Spirit of Vinegar; and from your 6 gallons, you will have only two gallons and two pints. If your spirit is still too weak, distill it even more often.

Keep this in a glass bottle, tightly stopped, to make your solvent <term>menstruum</term> <note>original: "Menstruum." A substance, usually a liquid, used to dissolve or extract materials.</note> with; you may seal it with cork and place leather over it.

You must provide three strong green glasses to make the *solvent* with, each having little mats around their bottoms and a capacity of four pints each.

To seal <term>lute</term> <note>original: "Lute." To seal a joint or a vessel airtight using a specialized paste or cement.</note> them: first, fit a *wooden* stopper—made of dry *wood* that has been boiled and then dried in an oven—into the mouth of the vessel. Then, melt hard wax to fill the cracks. Next, paste brown paper over that. Finally, prepare a sealing paste (luting) made of clay, horse dung, and ashes, and spread it over everything.

<header>Dr. Francis Anthony's</header>

Provide two or three glass stills to distill the first infusions from the earthy matter <note>original: "Infusions on the Earth." This likely refers to the residue or solid matter remaining after a previous stage of the process.</note>. These should be made of green glass and hold three or four pints each.

The rule for all distillations <note>original: "Stillings."</note> is that you must paste brown paper to seal the joint where the head of the still meets the body. You must also paste the receiver and the "nose" <note>The "nose" or beak of the still is the outlet tube where the vapor exits to be condensed into the receiving vessel.</note> of the still together so that none of the potency <note>original: "strength." In this context, it refers to the volatile vapors that contain the medicinal properties.</note> escapes.

Provide about a dozen calcining pots <term>calcining pots</term> <note>Pots made of ceramic or clay used for calcination, the process of heating a substance to very high temperatures to change its physical or chemical constitution.</note>. Many of these will break when they are placed in a strong fire; if that happens, you must lower the heat <note>original: "let your Fire slack."</note>.

--- Page 99 ---
<meta>This page begins a treatise by Bernard, Count of Trevisan (1406–1490), a famous Italian alchemist. The catchword "Never-" on the previous page's footer suggests a transition into the main text or a preceding section not included here. This page introduces the four essential steps of the alchemical process.</meta>

# A Unique Treatise by Bernard, Count of Trevisan, Concerning the Philosophers' Stone

Considering the long-held desires and hopes of students of the chemical art <note>original: "Chymick Art"</note>, I will briefly and openly explain this art in the present treatise. First, one must know the subject of the art; second, its foundation; third, its progress; and fourth and lastly, the extraction of the elements. Once these are known, anyone may very easily reach the goal of this art.

The subject of this admired science is *Sol* <note>original: "Sol" (the Sun), signifying gold in alchemical symbolism</note> and *Luna* <note>original: "Luna" (the Moon), signifying silver</note>, or rather, Male and Female. The Male is hot and dry, while the Female is cold and moist. Know for certain that our Stone is not composed of any other thing, even though many philosophers name various other materials about which they speak sophistically <term>sophistically: using clever but misleading arguments to conceal the true meaning from the uninitiated</term>.

<summary>Bernard of Trevisan introduces his treatise on the Philosophers' Stone, identifying its primary subjects as gold (Sol) and silver (Luna), represented as male and female principles.</summary>
<keywords>Bernard Count Trevisan, Philosophers' Stone, Alchemy, Sol and Luna, Chemical Art, Extraction of Elements, Alchemical Theory</keywords>

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page continues the treatise by Bernard, Count of Trevisan, specifically transitioning from the "Subject" of alchemy to its "Foundation" and "Progress." The author references several medieval authorities to emphasize that alchemical writings are often intentionally misleading to keep secrets from the uninitiated.</meta>

-> Bernard Trevisan <-

Nevertheless, by the writings of Scotus, Hortulanus, St. Thomas, Christopher of Paris <note>original: "Christopher Parisiensis"; a 15th-century alchemist</note>, and many others, many other things are deceptively <term>sophistically: using clever but misleading arguments to conceal the true meaning from the uninitiated</term> listed for different reasons. This is done so that ignorant men may be deceived, because it is not fitting for fools to know our secrets. This is what I thought appropriate to propose at this time concerning the subject of our art.

The foundation of this art is the knowledge of the four qualities <note>The four qualities are Hot, Cold, Moist, and Dry; the building blocks of matter in Aristotelian physics</note>. At the beginning of the work, coldness and moisture have the dominion. For as Scotus <note>Likely Michael Scot (c. 1175–1232) or a pseudo-epigraphical work attributed to him</note> says, just as the sun dries up the abundance of water in swampy and boggy places, in the same manner our Sulphur — when it is joined with its *Water* or Mercury — gradually consumes and drinks up that moisture by the help of the fire and by the assistance of the only living God.

The progress is nothing else than a certain action of opposites. For the description of opposite things is one and the same; if you achieve this balance <note>original: "equality"</note> twice, you shall finish the entire progress.

<sig>F 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: But</meta>

<summary>Bernard explains that the foundation of alchemy lies in balancing the four qualities, specifically using "Sulphur" and "Mercury" to dry out excess moisture, while warning that authorities often use deceptive language to hide these secrets from the unworthy.</summary>
<keywords>Scotus, Hortulanus, Christopher of Paris, four qualities, Sulphur and Mercury, alchemical secrecy, evaporation analogy, balancing opposites</keywords>

--- Page 101 ---
<meta>This page continues the instructions on the alchemical process, shifting from the theory of the four qualities to the practical "extraction" of the elements from their "root" (the combined principles of Sulphur and Mercury).</meta>

### Of the Philosopher's Stone

But now, all skill consists in drawing out the elements; therefore, read over what follows so often until you can conceive and understand it. Know that no one has ever spoken as plainly as I have in this place, as you will find by what follows. Therefore, give thanks to the great God, and be grateful to your friend who shared this treatise <note>original: "Tractate"</note> with you. Live also according to God and reason, because Divine *Wisdom* will not enter into a wicked soul, nor into a body subjected to sins.

The extraction of the elements is a certain combination of Blackness, Whiteness, Yellowness, and Redness <note>These four colors represent the traditional stages of the alchemical Great Work: *nigredo* (blackening), *albedo* (whitening), *citrinitas* (yellowing), and *rubedo* (reddening)</note>. Know that "natures" <note>In this context, "natures" refers to the essential spiritual qualities or "souls" of the metals</note> ought to be drawn from their root. This root is a certain gathering of elements, consisting of <term>Sulphur</term> and <term>Mercury</term>, which they call a "confused mass" <note>original: "confused Mass"; likely referring to the *massa confusa* or *prima materia*, the raw, chaotic starting material of the work</note>. 

The "natures" which are drawn out from the root are <term>Sulphur</term> and <term>Mercury</term>; when these are joined together, they are then separated and purified so that they may be better mingled afterward and united with the "body" out of which they were first drawn. After the colors have passed, and that which is above is made like that which is below, and that which is below is made like that which is above <note>This is a famous maxim from the *Emerald Tablet*, a foundational hermetic text: "That which is below is like that which is above, and that which is above is like that which is below, to do the miracles of one only thing"</note>, then miracles will appear from that process. Once this is done, you have a trian—

<meta>The text cuts off here at the end of the page; the catchword "gle" indicates the next page begins with "triangle."</meta>

<summary>Trevisan explains that the "extraction of elements" involves purifying Sulphur and Mercury from a "confused mass" and guiding them through four color stages to achieve a miraculous union. He emphasizes that this secret knowledge requires both repetitive study and a virtuous, God-fearing life.</summary>

<keywords>extraction of elements, Sulphur, Mercury, confused mass, alchemical colors, Emerald Tablet, alchemical stages, moral virtue</keywords>

--- Page 102 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the extraction of the elements, completing a geometric metaphor (a triangle in a quadrangle) and moving into the stage of "Multiplication" and the timeframe required for the Great Work.</meta>

...triangle in a quadrangle, and a fifth thing which is contained in four <note>This refers to the "Quintessence" or the fifth element, which alchemists believed was the pure essence extracted from the four classical elements (earth, air, fire, and water)</note>.

Now there remains the **Multiplication**, in which this must be briefly noted: that the **Elixir** <note>The Elixir is the finalized alchemical substance used for transmutation or medicinal healing</note> ought to be nourished from the same things from which it first had its composition. No philosopher before now has so openly declared this as I have here, and that for two reasons. First, because a long time is required from the beginning to the end of the work, although some philosophers say the **Stone** <term>Philosopher's Stone</term> may be made in one day, and others in one month. But know that they speak enigmatically <note>original: "Enigmatically"; speaking in riddles or symbolic language to hide secrets from the uninitiated</note>, and their words should not be understood literally in that way.

Nevertheless, I say with *Scotus* <note>Likely referring to Michael Scot (c. 1175–1232), a medieval scholar and polymath often associated with alchemical legends</note> that the Stone or "perfect work" may be made in one year. Secondly, because man’s life is short, and he grows old before he comprehends and understands what is necessary to be done in the composition of the Stone. Therefore, I have explained all things here so openly, lest this noble science should be lost and perish.

->*The Theory of the same Author.*<-

Use venerable Nature, for the philosophers, by their own authority, have imposed various names on this Nature, by reason... <note>The text continues on the next page: "by reason of..."</note>

<summary>Trevisan explains the stage of Multiplication, emphasizing that the Elixir must be nourished by its original components and clarifying that the Work typically takes one year despite the metaphorical "one day" claims of other authors.</summary>
<keywords>Multiplication, Elixir, Philosopher's Stone, Scotus, Alchemical Timeframe, Nature, Bernard Trevisan</keywords>

# Of the Philosopher's Stone

...reason of various colors appearing in its alteration <note>The text continues from the previous page: "the philosophers... have imposed various names on this Nature, by reason of..."</note>. For when it appears in the form of water, they have called it quicksilver <note>original: "Argent vive"</note>, permanent water, lead, spirit, spittle of the Moon <note>original: "Lune"; often referring to a silvery or mercurial liquid state</note>, tin, and so forth. And when it is made dry and becomes white, they have named it silver, magnesia, and white sulphur. And when it grows red, they call the same gold and ferment <note>In alchemy, the "ferment" is the substance that triggers the final transformation of a base metal into gold</note>. 

But they do not vary in the thing itself, as it is always only one thing and the same matter, and always of the same nature. Nothing enters into it which is not drawn from it, or which is not closely related to its nature. And this is most true: that is to say, the Stone is one, and it is one medicine <term>Medicine</term>. It is a water that is clear, bright, permanent, pure, and shining, with a celestial <note>original: "Celestical"; meaning heavenly or sky-blue</note> color. 

And if water did not enter into our medicine, it could not purify or improve itself, and so you could not obtain your desire. But that which improves it is the Sun <note>original: "Sol"; the alchemical term for gold or the solar principle</note>, for the water cannot be made better without it. For without the Sun and his shadow, a coloring poison <note>original: "tinging Poyson"; a potent substance used to "tinge" or dye base metals into gold</note> cannot be generated. Whoever, therefore, shall think that a tincture <term>Tincture</term> can be made without these two bodies—namely the Sun and the Moon <note>original: "Sol and Lune"; gold and silver</note>—proceeds to the practice like one who is blind. For body does not act upon body, nor spirit upon spirit; neither does form...

<sig>F 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: re-</meta>

<summary>This page explains that while the Philosopher's Stone is called by many names (such as lead, silver, or quicksilver) depending on its color and state, it remains a single substance that requires both "Sol" (gold) and "Lune" (silver) to be perfected.</summary>
<keywords>Philosopher's Stone, alchemy, Sol and Lune, Argent vive, Tincture, alchemical colors, transmutation, chemical medicine</keywords>

--- Page 104 ---
<meta>This page continues Bernard Trevisan's discourse on the nature of the Philosopher's Stone, specifically focusing on the relationship between matter, form, and spirit, and how they interact to create the final "gummy" substance of the Stone.</meta>

# Bernard Trevisan

...[cannot] receive an impression from form, nor matter from matter, because like does not exercise either action or passion <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, "action and passion" refer to the way one thing acts upon another and how that other thing is affected.</note> upon its like. For one is not more worthy than another; therefore, there can be no action between them, since like does not rule over like. 

But a body does receive an impression from a spirit, just as matter does from its form, and a spirit from its body, because they were made and created by God so that they might act upon and be affected by each other. For matter would flow infinitely if a form did not restrain and stop its flow <note>original: "Flux"</note>. Therefore, when the body acts as a form that "informs" <term>Informing</term>, it shapes and retains the spirit so that it cannot flow any longer.

The body, therefore, dyes <note>original: "tinge"</note> the spirit, and the spirit penetrates the body. While one body cannot penetrate another body, a subtle, spiritual, and congealed substance *can* penetrate and give color to the body. And this is that gummy and oily <note>original: "Oleaginous"</note> Stone, balanced in its natures, which contains a spiritual nature hidden within itself along with the purified elements. 

Therefore, the Philosopher's Stone must be completely reduced into this gummy state by the final repetition <note>original: "Reiteration"</note> or softening <term>Inceration</term> through a certain gentle flow <note>original: "Flux"</note>, dissolving all the elements so that they flow like wax. But when it is the Stone...

<summary>Trevisan explains the alchemical relationship between body and spirit, arguing that the Philosopher's Stone must reach a "gummy" or wax-like consistency where the spirit and body have perfectly integrated.</summary>
<keywords>Bernard Trevisan, Alchemy, Matter and Form, Spirit and Body, Flux, Inceration, Gummineſs, Tingeing, Elements</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Bernard Trevisan's explanation of the physical and spiritual properties of the Philosopher's Stone, specifically how the "gummy" Stone behaves like a metallic spirit.</meta>

<page-num>89</page-num>

### Of the Philosopher's Stone

...Stone, it appears like copper, even though it is actually a certain spiritual substance, penetrating and coloring <note>original: "tinging"</note> all metallic bodies.

From this, you may easily guess that this does not result from the density <note>original: "crassitude"</note> and coarseness of the Earth, but from a spiritual metallic substance which penetrates and enters. Therefore, you must resolve the body into a subtle metallic spirit, and afterward solidify and fix it, and then soften it <term>Inceration</term> so that it may flow before it transforms other metals. For gold colors nothing besides itself unless its own spirit is first extracted from its "belly" and made spiritual.

And know that our Mercurial Water <note>In alchemy, "Mercurial Water" refers to a specialized solvent, often called "living water," used to break down metals into their primary components.</note> is a living water and a burning fire, killing and tearing gold into pieces more effectively than common fire. Therefore, the more it is mixed, rubbed, and ground with the gold, the more it destroys the metal, and the living, fiery water becomes more refined <note>original: "attenuated"</note>. 

But when three are made one in the form of a solidified substance, then it possesses a true "tincture" <term>Tincture</term> which can endure the violence of the fire. Therefore, when the body is so colored, it can color another, and it possesses within itself all the power of transformation <note>original: "Tincture and Virtue"</note>. And from this, all those who transform with

<meta>catchword: Sol (Sun/Gold)</meta>

<summary>Trevisan describes the Philosopher's Stone as a spiritual metallic substance that must be made "spiritual" and flowable (incerate) to transform other metals. He explains that the "Mercurial Water" acts as a powerful solvent to break down gold and create a permanent, fire-resistant Tincture.</summary>

<keywords>Philosopher's Stone, Mercurial Water, Tincture, Inceration, Metallic Spirit, Gold, Alchemy, Bernard Trevisan</keywords>

--- Page 106 ---
<meta>This page continues Bernard Trevisan's explanation of the physical and spiritual properties of the Philosopher's Stone, specifically how the "gummy" Stone behaves like a metallic spirit.</meta>

<page-num>90</page-num>

# Bernard Trevisan

*Gold* <note>original: "Sol"</note> and his Shadow—namely <note>original: "viz."</note> with the poison that is *quicksilver* <note>original: "Argent vive"</note>—do perfectly complete our Stone, which we call the great and perfect Gum. And know for certain that it is not necessary for our Stone or Gum to lose its original mercurial nature during the purification <term>Sublimation</term> of its raw and primary spirit. For the oil and gum belonging to this Stone are nothing other than the elements themselves transformed into a mercurial state <note>original: "Mercurialized"</note> and made equal to one another. They are sealed up and solidified <note>original: "coagulated"</note>, yet remain capable of being dissolved and are "living." They are held or bound within the thickness <note>original: "viscosity"</note> of the "oily earth" and are inseparably mixed. 

We ought to know that this gum or oil is first drawn out of the "bodies" <note>In alchemy, "bodies" usually refers to solid metals like gold or silver.</note>. When it is added, it is reduced into a spirit until the excess moisture <note>original: "superfluous humidity"</note> of the "water" is turned into "air." By drawing one element out of another through gentle, consistent heat <term>Digestion</term>, the form of "water" is converted into the nature of "oil." Thus, in the end, our Stone receives the names of "Gum" and "Sulphur."

But whoever has brought the Stone this far—so that it appears like a blendable gum and allows itself to be mixed with all imperfect metals <note>original: "Bodies"</note>—he truly has found a great secret of Nature, because that is a perfect Stone, Gum, and Sulphur.

<summary>Trevisan explains that the Philosopher's Stone is a "perfect gum" created by combining gold with mercurial "poison" through a process of balanced heating. This substance remains mercurial and "living," eventually reaching a state where it can be blended with and transform imperfect metals.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, Bernard Trevisan, Gold, Sol, Quicksilver, Argent vive, Philosopher's Stone, Sublimation, Digestion, Sulphur, Elements</keywords>

--- Page 107 ---
<meta>This page continues Bernard Trevisan’s discussion on the composition of the Philosopher’s Stone, moving from its "gummy" physical state to the dual nature of its internal substances: the "Male" and "Female" principles.</meta>

<page-num>91</page-num>

### Of the Philosopher's Stone.

This Stone, then, is composed of a Body and a Spirit, or of a volatile and a fixed substance. This is necessary because nothing in the world can be created or brought to light without these two substances—namely, a Male and a Female. From this, it appears that although these two substances are not of the same species, a single Stone arises from them. And although they appear and are said to be two different substances, in truth they are but one: specifically, *quicksilver* <note>original: "Argent-vive"</note>. 

Within this *quicksilver*, a certain part is "fixed" and matured <term>digested</term>; it is Masculine, hot, dry, and provides the secret "form" or blueprint. The other part, which is the Female, is volatile, raw, cold, and moist. From these two substances, the whole process may easily be known, and the nature of the Stone entirely understood. 

Therefore, if our Stone consisted of only one substance, there could be no action or reaction <note>original: "Action and passion"</note> of one thing toward the other. One part would neither touch, approach, nor enter into the other. For example, a stone and a piece of wood have no effect on each other since they consist of entirely different materials; hence they cannot be mixed together in the slightest degree. The same logic applies to all things that differ in their basic matter. Wherefore it is evident and certain that both an "Agent" and a "Patient" <note>In alchemy, the "Agent" is the active force (Male) that acts upon the "Patient" (Female), the passive substance that receives the action.</note> are necessary...

<meta>catchword: ent</meta>

<summary>Trevisan explains that the Philosopher's Stone is a duality within a unity, composed of masculine (fixed/hot) and feminine (volatile/cold) principles derived from a single source: quicksilver.</summary>
<keywords>Philosopher's Stone, Argent-vive, quicksilver, Male and Female principles, volatile, fixed, Agent and Patient, alchemy theory</keywords>

--- Page 108 ---
<meta>This page continues Bernard Trevisan's explanation of the relationship between the alchemical principles of matter and form, leading into the practical application of these theories through the four elements. It completes the thought that the two necessary principles (Agent and Patient) must share a common origin.</meta>

<page-num>92</page-num>

sufficient to be of one and the same genus <note>original: "Genus"; a general category or classification</note>, but of a different species, just as a man differs from a woman. For although they agree in one and the same genus, they nevertheless have different operations and qualities, just like matter and form. For matter is passive <note>original: "suffereth"</note>, and form is active, transforming <note>original: "assimilating"</note> the matter into its own likeness. In this way, matter naturally thirsts for a form, just as a woman desires a husband, or as a lowly <note>original: "Vile"</note> thing desires a precious one, or an impure thing a pure one. Likewise, **quicksilver** <note>original: "Argent vive"</note> covets **sulphur**, as that which should make perfect that which is imperfect. So also a body freely desires a spirit, by which it may at length arrive at its perfection. Therefore, learn the natural foundations <note>original: "Roots"</note>, and the superior ones, with which you ought to refine <note>original: "reduce"</note> your matter, so that you may perfect your work. For this blessed Stone has in it all things necessary for its own perfection.

### The Practical Method <note>original: "Practick"</note> of the same Author.

If we carefully consider the words of **Morienus** <note>Morienus Romanus, a legendary 7th-century alchemist and hermit</note>, that great philosopher of **alchemy**, who says: "Mix together water, earth, air, and fire in a proper weight, and without doubt you will obtain all the secrets of—"

<summary>Trevisan explains how the passive "matter" and active "form" of the Stone interact like male and female to reach perfection, then begins a practical instruction based on balancing the four elements.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, matter and form, quicksilver, sulphur, Morienus, four elements, transmutation, Bernard Trevisan</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Bernard Trevisan's practical instructions on alchemy, moving from the theory of the four elements to their specific manipulation in the "divine science" of creating the Stone.</meta>

<page-num>93</page-num>

# On the Philosophers' Stone

...this divine science. And first, when he says, "put into the water," or "putrefy the earth in water," this signifies nothing else than the extraction of water out of earth, and the pouring of water upon the earth until the earth putrefies and is cleansed; otherwise, it would not bring forth its fruit. 

Secondly, when he says, "mix water and air," it is no more than if he had said, "mix the water now prepared with dissolved air," or "join and mix together dissolved air with water." Judge for yourselves, for you know that air is warm and moist, and you have the saying of <term>Morienus</term> <note>Morienus Romanus, a legendary 7th-century alchemist</note> concerning the dissolution of air, earth, fire, and water. 

Some, when they speak of dissolution, say that the solution of the fire is better, because whatever is dissolved in the fire flows into the air. And note that the fire of the <term>philosophers</term> <note>Alchemists often referred to themselves as "philosophers" and their specific chemicals as "philosophical" versions of common substances</note> is nothing else than air dissolved and congealed. You may better understand this through a comparison <note>original: "similitude"</note>: suppose that first you have air dissolved and congealed, to which you then add fire. The earth ought to be prepared first, and the fire dissolved, before they are mixed.

For the earth, together with the fire, ought to be put into a suitable vessel, and afterwards the inextinguishable

<meta>The catchword "Fire" indicates the next page begins with that word, completing the phrase "inextinguishable fire."</meta>

<summary>Trevisan interprets the symbolic language of alchemy, explaining that instructions to mix elements actually refer to the chemical extraction, purification, and recombination of substances in a "philosophical" state.</summary>
<keywords>Bernard Trevisan, Morienus, four elements, dissolution, putrefaction, philosophers' fire, alchemical vessel, purification</keywords>

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>This page concludes the treatise by Bernard Trevisan, completing the sentence from the previous page regarding the "inextinguishable fire."</meta>

<page-num>94</page-num>

<header>Bernard Trevisan, etc.</header>

...inextinguishable fire of nature. When this fire descends upon the earth, it consumes everything along with its gum <note>original: "Gumm"; in alchemy, "gum" often refers to the viscous or resinous state of the "prime matter" before it is purified</note> and transforms it into its own nature. Therefore, if you carefully consider the sayings and instructions <note>original: "Precepts"</note> of the philosophers <note>the alchemists</note> and understand their mystical meaning <note>original: "Sence"</note>, you will arrive at all the secrets of the divine chemical art.

Repeat this work in every step as before, until you have brought either all, or the most part of the residue <note>original: "Feces"; in alchemy, the solid remains or dregs left after distillation or sublimation.</note> into our natural and blessed liquid. Put all of this liquid together with the first liquid or solvent <note>original: "Menstruum"</note> called the **Green Lion's Blood**. Then, place that combined liquid in a glass vessel for fourteen days to undergo putrefaction <note>A process of decomposition or digestion through gentle heat, intended to break down the substance.</note>. Afterward, proceed to the "Separation of Elements," for you now have all the "Fire of the Stone" within this, our blessed liquid—an essence which previously lay hidden in the residue. This is a secret which all the philosophers marvelously hide.

### *The Separation of the Elements, the first of which is Air, also called our Ardent-Water and our Attractive Water.*

Then, put all of the aforementioned putrefied solvent <note>original: "Menstruum"</note> into a still <term>still</term> <note>A distillation apparatus.</note> made of fine **Venice Glass** that is appropriate for the volume of the liquid. Then, place it in a water bath <note>original: "Balneo Mariæ"; a container of hot water used to provide steady, gentle heat.</note> and attach the receiver flask. This receiver must be of a great length so that the spirit does not escape <note>original: "respire"</note>. With very temperate heat, separate the elements from one another; the Element of Air will issue forth first, which is an oil.

## *Our Ardent Water or Attractive Water is made in this way.*

When all of the first Element <note>Referencing the separation of the four elements—Fire, Air, Water, Earth—from the initial substance.</note> has been distilled, then rectify it <term>rectify</term> <note>To purify a liquid by repeated distillation.</note> in another still suited for the purpose. That is to say, distill it seven separate times, and continue until it will completely burn up a linen cloth that has been dipped into it when it is held to a flame. This is then called our *rectified Ardent Water*, and it is also called our *Attractive Water*. Keep this very tightly sealed, for otherwise its spirit, which is very subtle <note>In alchemy, "subtle" describes a substance that is highly volatile, refined, or capable of penetrating other bodies.</note>, will evaporate away.

By frequently rectifying the *Ardent Water*, the element of **Air** <note>original: "Aer"</note> will appear as a white oil floating on top of the water, and a yellow oil will remain behind, which will also come over with a stronger fire. Place **sublimate** <term>sublimate</term> <note>Likely corrosive sublimate (mercuric chloride), a common alchemical reagent.</note>, crushed into small pieces, upon an iron plate; in the cold, it will dissolve into a liquid. Filter it then, and pour some of the *Ardent Water* over it. It will draw to itself all the **Mercury** in the form of a green oil floating on the surface. Separate this oil and place it into a retort <term>retort</term> <note>A glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling substances.</note>. Distill first a water, and afterward, a thick green oil will come over, which is the **Oil of Mercury**.

->*The Flood or Water of the Stone.*<-

Then draw out the "Flood" or "Water of the Stone" by itself into another receiving vessel <note>original: "Receptory"</note>. This liquid will be somewhat white. Distill it with a very gentle heat from a water bath <note>original: "Balneo" (Balneum Mariae); a method of heating a vessel by placing it in warm water to ensure a steady, low temperature.</note>, until there remains at the bottom of the still a thick, oily substance like liquid pitch <note>A dark, viscous substance similar to tar or resin.</note>. ->*Our "Man's Blood" is thus made and rectified.*<-

Then pour our *Ardent Water* <note>Highly distilled, flammable alcohol or spirit used as a solvent.</note> upon that black and liquid matter. Stir them well together and let it stand, well-covered, for three hours. Then pour off the liquid <term>decant</term> and filter it. Add fresh *Ardent Water* and repeat this process three times. Afterward, distill it again with a moist, slow <note>original: "lent"</note> fire from a water bath, and do this three times. It is then called *rectified Man's Blood* <note>A metaphorical term for a highly purified alchemical substance, often associated with the life-force of the work.</note>, which those who work in the secrets of nature seek so diligently.

and so you have the elements exalted into the power <note>original: "virtue"</note> of their quintessence—specifically the "Flood" (which is Water) and the "Air." Let this "Blood" <note>Referring to the "Man's Blood" or spirit prepared on the previous page.</note> be kept for a period of time.

### *The Oil or Fire, or the Earth of the Stone*

Then pour the "Flood" or "Water" onto the black and soft matter, which is the "Earth of the Stone." ### *The Fiery Water*

Repeat <term>reiterate</term> this process three times. It shall then be called "Rectified Water of Fire." In this way, you have three of the elements exalted into the power of the quintessence: namely, Water, Air, and Fire.

### The Earth

Then calcine <note>To heat a substance to a high temperature, without melting it, to remove volatile parts and reduce it to a powder.</note> the Earth (which is black and dry) in a furnace of reverberation <note>A furnace in which the flame and heat are reflected from the roof onto the material being processed.</note> until it becomes a very fine white powder <note>original: "Calx"</note>.

### *The Water of Life, which is our Mercury and our Lunary*

Then mix the Fiery Water with this white powder <note>original: "Calx"</note> and distill it with a strong fire until it is all drawn off, just as before. Calcine the Earth that remains at the bottom of the still again, and then distill it once more with a strong fire as before, and calcine it again. Repeat this process of distilling and calcining seven times until the entire substance of the powder is lifted up through the alembic <note>original: "Limbeck"; the cap or head of a distilling apparatus.</note>. Then you have the "Water of Life" purified <note>original: "rectified"</note> and made truly spiritual; in this way, you have the four elements exalted into the power <note>original: "virtue"</note> of their quintessence <note>The "fifth essence," representing the purest and most concentrated form of a substance.</note>. This Water will dissolve all bodies, break them down <note>original: "putrify"</note>, and cleanse <note>original: "Purge"</note> them. This is our "Mercury" and our "Lunary," <note>Alchemical names for the universal solvent or the spiritual principle of the work.</note> and whoever thinks there is any other water than this is ignorant and a fool, and shall never be able to achieve the desired result <note>original: "effect"</note>.

### *A grand secret or Accurtation <note>original: "Accurtation"; a shortened alchemical process intended to produce results more quickly or cheaply than the full "Great Work."</note> of Sir George Ripley, for the help of those who have made the Philosophers' Mercury and whose poverty disables them from proceeding to either the Red or White Elixir*

Take the ceruse <note>original: "Cerus"; here referring to a white powder or "cream" produced from tin.</note> or cream of the finest and purest molten Cornish tin, and reduce it into a fine white powder <note>original: "Calx"</note>. Then distill that Mercury from the powder again, and soak <note>original: "inbibe"</note> the powder with it again. Repeat this distillation and soaking over and over until the powder becomes subtle and oily—indeed, so subtle that it will flow upon a fiery hot plate of copper like wax and not evaporate. 

This substance will then convert copper into fine silver, for the softness and weakness <note>original: "neshness"</note> of the tin is taken away by the benefit of our Mercury joined <note>original: "confixed"</note> to it. By virtue of this, it is made <term>indurate</term> <note>hardened or solidified.</note> and clean, so that it may be compatible with hard metals during melting <note>original: "fusion"</note> and in <term>malleation</term> <note>the process of being shaped or extended by hammering.</note> just like pure silver. This work is very profitable and easy to perform; use it, therefore, until you are rich, and then I pray you, for our Lord's sake, proceed to the "Great Work," which

### *The Oil, which is the Element of Fire, and our red Mercury.*

### *The digestion of the Red Stone.*

Then take out the white stone and divide it into two parts. Determine the true weight of each half. Reserve one half for the "white work" <note>the process of transmuting base metals into silver.</note>, and put the other half back into the glass vessel. Seal it up again with the *Seal of Hermes* <term>Hermes Seal</term> <note>an airtight seal made by melting the glass neck of a vessel shut.</note>. Then, move the glass into a *cinerition* <term>Cinerition</term> <note>original: "Cinerition"; a furnace or container of hot ashes used to provide steady, intense heat.</note>, which is a somewhat hotter fire. Let it stand there undisturbed in that stage of digestion until it becomes red and of a purple color. Now you have the red stone also ready to be fermented <note>the process of combining the "stone" with a noble metal (gold for red, silver for white) to "fix" its power and allow it to transmute other metals.</note>.

### *The Preparation of the Ferment for the white stone.*

Then take silver that has been well purified <note>original: "purged."</note> of all other metals and any other impurities <note>original: "filth."</note> that may be joined with it. Dissolve it in an equal amount of our *Lunary* <term>Lunary</term> <note>literally "belonging to the moon"; an alchemical solvent or "mercury" specifically used for silver.</note>, which is our *Mercury*, as the quantity of your silver is. Use no greater quantity of the solvent than silver, as near as you can manage. Place it upon warm ashes and keep it closely covered. When it is thoroughly dissolved, the entire liquid will be green. Then, *rectify* <note>to purify a liquid by repeated distillation.</note> our Mercury by drawing it away from the silver again two or three times, so that not a single drop of our Mercury is left with it. Then, seal up this *Oil of Luna* <note>original: "Oyl of Luna"; a liquefied, purified form of silver (Luna).</note> in a *chemie* <term>Chemia</term> <note>a chemical vessel or flask.</note>, and set—

### *The Fermentation of the White Stone*

Then, take the half of the white stone previously reserved for the "white work" <note>the process of making silver.</note> and put it into a suitable glass vessel. Note its weight. When they are thus fixed together and have become one very fine powder, <term>incerate</term> <note>a process of softening a solid substance by adding liquid until it has a waxy consistency.</note> it. That is to say, let it soak up the "white oil" of our stone—which is our <term>Lunary</term> <note>an alchemical solvent or "mercury" associated with silver.</note>—by pouring it on almost drop by drop until the stone becomes oily. In this manner, repeat <note>original: "iterate."</note> this work until the stone flows in the fire like wax when it is placed upon a red-hot plate of copper and does not evaporate. Finally, let it congeal until it is hard, white, and transparently clear.

### *The Preparation of the Red Ferment*

Then, likewise take **gold** that is very pure, having first been purged of all other **metals** that may be joined with it by using ten parts of **antimony**. For the **Red Work**, add as much of the previously mentioned **Gold Ferment** as equals one-fourth of the **Stone**. Then, **fix** them together <note>original: "fix"; the alchemical process of making a volatile substance stable under heat so it no longer evaporates.</note> using **fire** in a **fixing vessel** <note>original: "fixatory Vessel"</note>, just as you did with the **White Stone**. This will be completed successfully in two or three days.

### *The Process of Inceration for the Red Stone*

Once they are **fixed** together and have become a very fine **powder**, perform **inceration** <note>original: "inceration"; the process of softening a substance by gradually adding liquid and heating it until it has a waxy consistency.</note>. This means you must **imbibe** it <note>original: "imbibe"; to saturate or soak the solid matter with a liquid.</note> with the **Red Oil** of our **Stone**. Then, **congeal** it <note>solidify or thicken the substance through cooling or evaporation</note> again. Repeat this process of **imbibing** and **congealing** until the substance flows in the **fire** like **wax**, yet does not evaporate when placed on a red-hot **copper plate**. 

Finally, let it **congeal** until it is clear, transparent, hard, and red in color—resembling a **ruby** or a **jacinth** <note>original: "Jacinth"; a reddish-orange gemstone, also known as hyacinth.</note>. This is the **Medicine of the Third Degree** <note>the final and most potent stage of the alchemical medicine.</note> and the perfect **Red Stone**. It is capable of **transmuting** all **metallic bodies**—especially **mercury**, **lead**, and **silver**—into pure **gold**, as fine as any found in a natural mine.

In this way, you have the method for creating the **Philosopher's Stones**, both **White** and **Red**, which is the great secret of the **philosophers** <note>referring to alchemists, the "natural philosophers" of the era.</note>. These **Stones** must be kept separately in individual **glass containers** or clean boxes, stored in a warm or at least dry place, just as you would store sugar.

### *The Multiplication or Increase of the Virtue and Goodness of the Aforesaid White and Red Stone.*

If you desire to enhance your <term>Medicine</term> <note>original: "Medicine"; another name for the Philosopher's Stone or the elixir used for transmutation.</note> or <term>Stone</term> in quantity or quality, then put your aforementioned white or red Stone (or a portion of each) into a separate glass vial of an appropriate size. Seal the vial tightly, then hang your glass or glasses in a **vaporous Mary’s Bath** <note>original: "Balneo Mariæ vaporoso"; a type of alchemical water bath, named after Mary the Jewess, where the vessel is heated by steam rather than being submerged in water.</note> so that it does not touch the water. 

In this warm vapor or "breath," the Stone—which was previously solidified in the glass—will now be dissolved. Afterward, solidify it again upon warm ashes. Continue to dissolve and solidify it in this way, repeating this work of dissolution and solidification until, at last, the dissolved Stone within the glass will solidify as soon as it comes out of the pot or water bath and feels the cold air, without any other method of solidification being needed. 

Note that every time you dissolve and solidify your Medicine or Stone in this process, its power increases tenfold in <term>projection</term> <note>original: "projection"; the final stage of the alchemical process where the "Medicine" is cast upon a base metal to transmute it into gold or silver.</note>. ### The Way of Projection.

Because it is very cumbersome to melt a million parts of any substance <note>original: "Body"; in alchemy, "body" refers to the metallic base or physical matter being transformed.</note> at once, do the following when you wish to perform a <term>projection</term> <note>original: "Projection"; the act of casting the alchemical medicine onto base metal to transmute it into gold or silver.</note>. Again, for the third time, project one ounce of this solidified <note>original: "congealed"</note> mercury onto another 100 ounces of washed mercury, and all shall be converted into Medicine.

# The Mirror of Alchemy <note>original Latin: *Speculum Alchymiæ*</note>, the True Glass <note>In this context, "glass" means a mirror or a looking-glass, intended to reflect the truth of the subject.</note> of Alchemy by Roger Bacon.

I salute and greet you, most dearly beloved. This is the Mirror of Alchemy, which I have imagined in my heart and drawn from the books of wise men. It fully contains all that they have gathered regarding the perfection of alchemy. I present it to you personally; in it, all things required for this art are gathered together, including those which were dispersed in various places. 

I shall respond to your prudence and wisdom in this way: all things are created from the four elements <note>The classical elements: earth, water, air, and fire.</note>, and they are the roots and the matter of all things. The diversity of things consists of three qualities—that is to say, color, taste, and smell. To me, there are only three <note>Though the author says "three," he proceeds to list four determining factors.</note> causes of variety: the different elements, different proportions, different **decoctions** <term>decoctions</term> <note>original: "Decoctions"; in alchemy, this refers to the process of refining, purifying, or "ripening" a substance through steady heat, similar to slow boiling.</note>, and different **mixtures**. Therefore, if you wish to transform one metallic body into another, you must know the

Nature of one contrary and of another in every diversity; and when you know this, then you may, by addition and subtraction, add more of one element and less of another, and boil <note>original: "seeth"; to cook or refine through heat</note> them together well or poorly, and also mix them together well or poorly according to your own will and desire. 

And a man may do this effectively with metals if he knows, without error, how to separate the elements—that is to say, how to reduce them to their "first matter" and root. This root is brimstone and quicksilver (also called sulfur and mercury <note>original: "☿"; the alchemical symbol for the element mercury.</note>), and that is the root or matter that is nearest to the source. 

But because the separation of elements in metals is difficult and arduous, the masters sought a way to obtain the nearest roots without such labor by using brimstone and quicksilver directly. From these, they performed their separation of elements, which they used; they stated that only these elements belong within metals, and that foreign elements from other things—such as blood, eggs, and hair—do not enter the metal except through the power or mixture of them with the aforementioned elements drawn from metallic spirits and bodies.

### Of Alchemy <note>original Latin: *Alchymiæ*</note>

Separation—we must find the skill <note>original: "cunning"</note> or knowledge by which we may do it more quickly; but we cannot do this in any way unless we add to them things that are diverse and contrary. For you must separate the elements by their opposites <note>original: "contraries"</note> through our knowledge and mastery. Therefore, when two contrary things are mixed together, one acts upon the other and thus causes it to yield a part of its constitution <note>original: "Complexion"; the specific balance of qualities like heat or moisture</note> and power <note>original: "Virtue"</note>. 

For this reason, you must first learn to know the constitution and properties of all things before you attempt <note>original: "enterprise"</note> to create a mixture <note>original: "commixtion"</note> of them in their proper natures. It is necessary that you understand the works of Nature which you intend to perform: how much and what each thing contributes of its nature and constitution, and how much and what it lacks of another constitution and nature through the process you are performing and by the nature of the contrary things which you mix together. 

If you err in any of these, you must know how much and in what way you have erred; for if you know this, then you know how to rectify anything in the world and how to reduce anything to its "first matter" <note>the fundamental, unformed substance of all things</note> or constitution, or to any other thing according to your desire. Conversely, if you do not know this, you should not attempt to interfere, except by means of certain things

# Alchemy
<note>original: "Alchymia"</note>

The second is, that you know how to mix <note>original: "commix"</note> your prepared materials well together, which is to take various and diverse things and make one substance that is inseparable forever. For if you do not know how to mix your materials well and naturally—so that every thing is destroyed and brought first back to its own primary being <note>the fundamental "first matter"</note> and proper species, and then one new thing is generated from them—whatever you have done is worth nothing. You must also know the sign that indicates when your mixing <note>original: "mixtion"</note> is completed.

The third is, that you know the certain proportion—that is, the specific quantity—of those materials that you ought to mix together, and also to know by reason why it should be so, so that you may be sure to find the result you seek. Depending on the quantities you have mixed into your melted bodies <note>the base metals being transformed</note>, the result will fade away either slowly or quickly, regardless of how well the things were prepared. Unless they were mixed together according to knowledge and nature, you have lost all your labor as far as the final completion <note>original: "complement"</note> is concerned. This failure will be clearly perceived during the examination <note>the testing of a metal's purity, often by fire</note> thereof, when the transmuted body is put to examination in

The wise man says that glass purges corrupt metallic bodies <term>Metalline Bodies</term> and cleanses them. For glass makes metallic bodies that are difficult to melt <note>original: "hard Fusion"</note> become soft in their melting <term>fusion</term>, and this is a secret.

And with salt, bodies are calcined <term>calcined</term> and dried; for salt cleanses the bodies insofar as it dries up the sulfur <term>Sulphur</term> which is in them. It is because of this moisture <note>original: "humidity"</note> that they stink and are black and combustible <note>original: "burnable"</note>. For calcined bodies are clean, enduring the fire without stinking, and this is a great secret. But know that this is spoken concerning another secret, which I will not show here, nor will I write of it, for it is the "Secret of Secrets." By that secret, when it is well and perfectly known, a man may arrive at the secrets of all other kinds. Of this secret, I have shown you part; and if you do not understand what remains, I will declare no more, either by tongue or pen.

# The *Wonderful Effectiveness and Almost Incredible Power of True Oil Made from* NATURAL SULFUR <note>original: "Sulphur Vive," referring to raw or native sulfur</note>, *Set on Fire, and Commonly Called Oil of* SULFUR *by the Bell Method* <note>original: "per Campanam"; a process where burning sulfur vapors are collected under a glass bell-shaped vessel.</note>

provide for them while he lived, and concluded that should he die, they would be forced to beg for bread from door to door. Thereupon he came (says *Helmont*) and requested from me something for the preservation of his life. 

I then (being a young man) pitied his sad condition and thought to myself: the fumes of burning *sulfur* are found by experience to be powerfully effective at preserving *wines* from spoiling <note>original: "corruption"</note>. Then, recollecting my thoughts, I concluded that the acid liquid or *oil*—which is made from *natural sulfur* <note>original: "Sulphur Vive"</note> when set on fire—must of necessity contain this fume within itself. Indeed, it contains the entire scent of the sulfur, insofar as it is nothing else but the very *sulfurous* fumes absorbed <note>original: "imbibed"</note> or drunk up by its *mercurial salt* <note>In alchemical theory, the "mercurial salt" refers to the fluid, life-giving essence or mineral principle within a substance.</note>, and so it becomes a condensed liquid. 

Then I thought to myself: since our blood is to us nothing other than the "wine of our life," if it were preserved, it would—if not prolonging life itself—at least keep it healthy and free from those many diseases that originate from decay <note>original: "corruption"</note>. By this means, the life being sound, free from diseases, and defended from pain and suffering, might in some way be spun out to a greater length than it would be otherwise.

<meta>This page continues the anecdote from Jan Baptista van Helmont regarding a man who used "Oil of Sulfur" to maintain his health and longevity.</meta>

### Oil of Sulfur

...how to make it for whenever he might need it afterward. I advised him regarding this liquid <note>original: "liquor"</note> that he should take two drops before each meal in a small drink of beer. He was not to exceed that dose normally, nor was he to stop using it. I assumed <note>original: "taking for granted"</note> that two drops of that oil contained a large amount of the smoke <note>original: "fume"</note> of sulfur. 

The man followed my advice, and as of today in the year 1641, he is vigorous <note>original: "lusty"</note> and in good health. He walks the streets of Brussels without any complaints and is likely to live even longer. What is most remarkable is that in this entire span of forty-one years, he was never so ill that he had to stay in bed. In fact, even though he broke his leg near the ankle bone from a fall on the ice in the middle of winter at a very advanced age, he recovered using this oil without the slightest symptom of a fever. 

Although in his old age poverty had reduced him to great distress <note>original: "straits"</note> and hardship—leaving him lacking many things necessary for the comfort and convenience of life—he still lives healthily and soundly, though he is thin and lean. 

The old man's name is **John Mass**. He was a servant to **Rithovius**, the Bishop of **Ypres**, and attended him in the chamber where the Earls of **Hoorn** and **Egmont** were beheaded by the **Duke of Alva** <note>The execution of these noblemen in 1568 was a pivotal event in the Dutch Revolt against Spanish rule.</note>. He was twenty-five years old at that time; therefore, he is now a full ninety-nine years of age, healthy and vigorous, and still continues to use that liquid daily.

<summary>Van Helmont recounts the case of John Mass, who, after taking a daily dose of "Oil of Sulfur" for forty-one years, reached the age of ninety-nine in remarkably good health despite poverty and injury.</summary>

<meta>This page continues George Starkey’s discussion of the medicinal and preservative properties of "Oil of Sulfur," building on the previous account of John Mass and Jan Baptist van Helmont.</meta>

# George Starkey’s

So far, **Van Helmont**’s <note>Jan Baptist van Helmont (1580–1644) was a pioneering Flemish chemist and physician.</note> account is most remarkable; it provides the philosophical reasoning behind the advice on which his practice was grounded. Elsewhere, the same author relates how, by using this liquid <note>original: "Liquor"</note>, he cured many dangerous and tragic fevers which other doctors had given up on as hopeless. In other places, he commends it as a peerless remedy to soothe the insatiable thirst that accompanies most fevers.

To the account and testimony of this most learned doctor and sharp philosopher, I shall add my own experience.

I find it to be an extraordinary preservative against decay <note>original: "corruption"</note>, not only in living creatures but even in dead meat, beer, wine, ale, and so on. It is a restorer of "dying" beer and wines that have soured, and a cure for beer when it is "sick" or "roping" <note>“Roping” refers to a type of bacterial spoilage in beer that causes the liquid to become viscous and form long, slimy threads or "ropes."</note>. By this means, meat may be preserved so incorruptibly that no embalming in the world can surpass it for the keeping of a corpse, nor can salting come near its effectiveness for conserving meat, poultry, or fish. Through this process, these foods are not only kept from decaying but are made into a "mummified balsam" <note>original: "mumial Balsome"; in alchemical medicine, this refers to a substance that preserves the body's vital essence and prevents rot.</note>, which is itself a preservative against decay for those who eat it. However, because this is a rare curiosity and too costly to be used as a 

<summary>Starkey validates Van Helmont’s claims about Oil of Sulfur as a fever remedy and adds his own observations on its power to preserve food, restore spoiled alcohol, and prevent biological decay.</summary>

--- Page 159 ---
<meta>This page continues George Starkey’s discussion of the practical and medicinal applications of "Oil of Sulfur" (sulfuric acid), transitioning from its use as a preservative for food and drink to its benefits for personal hygiene and respiratory health.</meta>

### Oil of Sulfur

common <note>original: "vulgar"</note> experiment, I shall pass it over and move on to those uses which are most beneficial and desirable.

It is an excellent cleanser of the teeth; when scoured with it, they will become as white as the purest ivory. When the mouth is washed with this oil dropped into water or white wine—making it only as sharp as vinegar—it prevents the growth of that yellow scale <note>calculus or tartar</note> which usually adheres to the teeth and is the forerunner of their decay <note>original: "putrefaction"</note>. It prevents future rot and stops existing rot from progressing further. It also takes away tooth pain, diverts discharge <note>original: "Rheums"; refers to watery secretions from the eyes, nose, or mouth</note>, and is a reliable remedy for strong-smelling breath, making it very sweet. In short, there is no more desirable substance to be found for those who want clean or sound teeth, sweet breath, or to be free from discharges. For this purpose, let the water be prepared by dropping this oil into it until it is as sharp as vinegar, as I mentioned before.

Against a tickling cough and hoarseness, it is an exceptional <note>original: "rare"</note> remedy. It is effective not only when taken internally—two or three drops twice a day in one's usual drink before each meal—but also when used as a throat gargle. Used this way, it is excellent for swollen throats and *tonsillitis or quinsy* <note>original: "Angina's"; in this era, "angina" referred to inflammatory throat conditions that caused choking or swelling</note>,

<meta>catchword: na's,</meta>

<summary>George Starkey describes Oil of Sulfur as a superior treatment for dental hygiene, bad breath, and respiratory issues like coughs and throat swelling.</summary>

...anginas <note>original: "na's"; completing the word "Anginas" from the previous page, referring to throat inflammations or quinsy</note>, glandular swellings <note>original: "Strumas"</note>, inflamed palates of the mouth, an inflamed uvula <note>original: "Vvula"</note>, or the tonsils <note>original: "Almonds of the Ears"</note>, which are usually said at those times to have "fallen." It is also excellent against headaches and for diverting discharges <note>original: "Rheums"</note> from the eyes when used to wash the temples. Likewise, it is a pleasant, safe, and effective remedy to remove skin eruptions <note>original: "Tetters"</note>, skin discolorations <note>original: "Morphew"</note>, the itch, or scabs when dropped into water.

In addition to these outward applications, it is a sovereign remedy <note>original: "a Lord"</note> when taken internally. It prevents corruption and roots out its seeds, no matter how deeply they are concealed in the body. Because of this, it opens long-standing <note>original: "inveterate"</note> obstructions, eliminates old pains, and prevents the usual relapses into pains caused by urinary difficulty <note>original: "Stranguretical"</note>, colic, or arthritis. It is cleansing <note>original: "absterfive"</note>, purging all waste-like <note>original: "Excrementitian"</note> deposits in the mesenteric vessels <note>original: "Mesaraick or Mesenterial"; refers to the membranes and vessels connecting the intestines to the abdominal wall</note>. By doing so, it cuts off the original progression and removes the cause of putrid corruption, which is the underlying source of very many diseases.

For this reason, it lengthens life and frees the body from many pains and ailments to which it would otherwise be prone.
