# Oil of Sulfur

It is a pleasant remedy, possessing only a slight sharpness that is very pleasing to the palate. Yet this acidity is quite different from the type of acidity that precedes rot <note>original: "putrefaction"</note>, which it kills and destroys. In the same way, the acidity of *oil of vitriol* <note>original: "Spirit of Vitriol"; sulfuric acid</note> is neutralized by the stable bitterness <note>original: "fixed Acrimony"</note> of its own *residue* <note>original: "Caput mortuum"; Latin for "dead head," the solid remains left after distillation</note>, or as the acidity of *vinegar* is neutralized by contact with *white lead* <note>original: "Cerusse"</note> or *red lead* <note>original: "Minium"</note>.

Abnormal <note>original: "Præternatural"</note> heat and thirst during *fevers* are never relieved as quickly or easily as they are by this remedy. Nor is there anything that can be taken more safely or beneficially over a long period. Only *spirit of salt* <note>original: "Spirit of Salt"; hydrochloric acid</note> (of the kind the noble Van *Helmont* <note>Jan Baptista van Helmont (1580–1644) was a foundational chemist and physician who influenced Starkey</note> describes) may be combined with it for safety and long-term benefit, especially for *kidney* <note>original: "Nephritical"</note> conditions and burning or painful *urination* <note>original: "sharpness of Urine"</note>.

Now, although this is such a noble medicine, there is none in the world more shamefully adulterated and counterfeited. Our "wise" doctors recommend as a substitute <note>original: "quid pro quo"; Latin for "one thing for another"</note> a corrupted mineral acidity made from *vitriol*, distilled in a *glass vessel* <note>original: "Retort"</note> from common *sulfur*. This is what the *renegade chemists* <note>original: "Apostate Chemists"</note> prepare and sell, and what *dishonest pharmacists* <note>original: "Knavish Apothecarries"</note> use and give to their patients instead of the true *spirit*. If genuine, the true spirit is as clear as water, heavy <note>original: "ponderous"</note>, and ex—

<sig>K</sig>
<meta>catchword: exquisitely</meta>

<summary>George Starkey describes Oil of Sulfur as a superior remedy for fevers and kidney ailments while warning readers about the prevalence of fake, low-quality versions sold by dishonest chemists and pharmacists.</summary>

--- Page 162 ---
<meta>This page continues George Starkey’s critique of common medical practices, specifically distinguishing between "Spirit of Vitriol" and his preferred "Spirit of Sulphur" (sulfuric acid produced from burning sulfur), arguing that the latter is a far superior preservative and medicine.</meta>

->Geo. Starkey's<-

...exquisitely acidic, made of **native sulfur** <note>original: "Sulphur Vive"</note> only, set on fire without any other mixture, and the fumes received in a broad glass, fitted for the purpose, commonly called a *Campana* or **Bell** <note>original: "Bell"; a bell-shaped glass vessel used for collecting acidic vapors</note>, named for its shape or likeness.

Most foolish <note>original: "sottish"</note> is that maxim of the doctors, that *spirit of sulfur* and *vitriol* <note>vitriol refers to sulfate minerals; Starkey is arguing that acid derived from sulfur is different from acid derived from iron or copper sulfate</note> are of the same nature. Experience teaches otherwise: the mere **acidity** <note>original: "Acetosity"</note> of vitriol (which carries over none of its excellent virtues) will dissolve **mercury** <note>original: "Argent Vive"; quicksilver</note>, which the strongest spirit of sulfur—if truly and not deceitfully made—will not touch. Nor will the acid from vitriol restore **beer** or **wines**, or preserve them, as spirit of sulfur will do. 

One, therefore, is an unripe, **corrosive acidity** <note>original: "Esurine Acetosity"; "esurine" means hungry or corrosive</note> of little virtue. The other is a **balm** <note>original: "Balsom"</note> of antidotal power, a preservative against corruption. On that account, nothing can be used more effectively as a preservative against, or a remedy for, *contagious fevers*, *smallpox*, *measles*, or *plague* <note>original: "Pestilence"</note> than this spirit of sulfur. 

Nothing is more ridiculous than the other acid <note>spirit of vitriol</note>, which, being drawn from common sulfur that has an infection of malignity mixed with it (which it took from the **arsenic-like** <note>original: "Arsenical"</note> nature of the minerals from which it was melted), adds no virtue to the raw spirits of vitriol. Instead, it only causes that which was previously of little virtue to become a medicine of more <meta>catchword: danger</meta>

<summary>George Starkey distinguishes between the "Spirit of Sulfur" and "Spirit of Vitriol," arguing that the former is a powerful preservative and medicine for fevers and plague, while the latter is corrosive and often contaminated with arsenic.</summary>

# Oil of Sulfur <note>original: "Oyl of Sulphur"</note>

...danger and risk, but not a bit more goodness than it had when first drawn from the vitriol <note>vitriol: a sulfate mineral from which acid was distilled; Starkey argues that some people simply color this common acid to sell it as the more valuable "spirit of sulfur"</note>. This acid, being naturally clear and raw, is tinted with some root or bark to deceive the ignorant by its color. Thus, the trusting world is tricked and cheated; instead of the most noble remedies that were promised by name, fake <note>original: "adulterated"</note> trifles are produced, to the discredit of the craft and the scandal and disgrace of the medical profession.

To reveal these abuses and defend true art, I have written my introduction <note>original: "Præludium"; a preliminary piece of writing or music</note> concerning this oil or spirit of sulfur. Its virtues (if truly and faithfully made) are so remarkably prominent and almost incredibly effective that I thought it worth my effort to share with the studious reader, in a few lines, both what real benefit can be expected from the true version and what harm is done to misled (if not destroyed) patients by fake <note>original: "Sophisticate"; adulterated or fraudulent</note> oil of sulfur.

That those who desire this so pleasant, so effective, and profitable a remedy, may

not be deceived by the low-quality counterfeit **Oil of Vitriol**, which is wrongly called **Oil of Sulfur** just because it was distilled once from common, unhealthy brimstone <note>original: "Brimstone"; an archaic name for sulfur</note> and tinted with some bark or root. The town and all the apothecary shops are full of this, which is a great abuse of the craft, but an even greater abuse of those who use it instead of the genuine medicine. In reality, it does not have a single quality like the true oil.

Let the reader be informed that at George Starkey’s house, in **St. Thomas the Apostle** <note>original: "St. Thomas Apostles"; a street and parish in the City of London</note>, next door to **Black-Lyon-Court**; and at Richard Johnson’s, at the **Globe** in **Montague Close** in **Southwark**, the genuine oil is available. It is extracted from native sulfur <note>original: "Sulphur Vive"; raw or naturally occurring sulfur</note> (by setting it on fire) without any additions except the sulfur itself.

This true oil is easily recognized by its clarity, sharpness, and weight. It does not react with mercury <note>original: "Quick-silver"</note>, and when applied to silver filings, it turns bitter like gall. It preserves wine and beer from spoiling and restores them when they have gone bad. In short, it quenches feverish heat and thirst, etc. As has been described in detail before, anyone can distinguish the true oil from that which is false and adulterated <note>original: "Sophisticate"</note>. At these two locations, you can be confident that you are getting the real and true medicine. Furthermore, at Richard Johnson’s house in **Montague Close** in **Southwark**, you may also find various **chemical salts, oils, and spirits.**

# Oil of Sulfur

In addition to this **Oil or Spirit of Sulfur**, several other rare and wonderfully effective medicinal secrets are available for the certain, safe, and fast cure of most (if not all) diseases. Their effectiveness has been proven by hundreds of patients who were judged to be incurable or in desperate danger by other doctors. These are the extraordinary secrets and preparations of George Starkey, who calls himself a ***Philosopher by the Fire*** <note>original: "Philosopher by the Fire"; a term used by alchemists to describe someone who learns the secrets of nature through laboratory experimentation with heat</note>.

In particular, this includes that ***Pill*** or ***Antidote*** that has been wrongly claimed as the invention of Mr. Richard Mathews. In truth, Mathews received the recipe for that preparation (for which he has since become so famous) from the aforementioned George Starkey, its true creator. Starkey received it from God through diligent research, without the help of any ***book*** or ***teacher***. He has since improved this preparation, increasing its potency far beyond the version Mr. Mathews received from him, a fact that has been, and continues to be, confirmed daily by the ***experience*** of capable ***men***. Regarding this ***antidote***, ***pill***, or rather ***anodyne elixir*** <note>original: "Anodinous Elixir"; a medicine used to alleviate pain</note>, its virtues, and its development into nearly a true ***universal remedy*** <note>original: "Universality"; a reference to a panacea or cure-all medicine</note> through four variations discovered by the original ***author*** through long ***experience***, Starkey has written about it in great detail. He describes how to administer it and how to care for the ***patient*** by

...one or all of those preparations for his recovery from any of the most desperately acute or fixed **chronic** <term>chronic disease</term> <note>original: "Cronical"; a long-lasting illness</note> diseases. This is written to inform those who have been wrongly and falsely convinced that only Mr. Richard Mathews and Paul Hobson possess the truly prepared version of that medicine. They have condemned all other versions as counterfeits, which is a slight and an obvious injury to the original inventor <note>George Starkey refers to himself here as the original inventor of the medicine Mathews made famous.</note>. He considers it unreasonable that someone who learned everything he knows from him should then judge the inventor as a fraud, simply because the inventor does not limit himself to that one specific preparation. He calls it his **common diaphoretic elixir** <note>original: "Elixir Diaphoretick, commune"; a medicine intended to induce sweating to break a fever</note>. Skilled and sensible practitioners, once they have used his more effective and more potent preparations of the same kind, have such a low opinion of this common version (compared to the others) that they no longer desire it.

<header>Oil of Sulphur <note>original: "Oyl of Sulphur"; a chemical preparation of sulfuric acid produced by burning sulfur under a glass bell</note></header>

*When this treatise and the postscript were written, Mr. Starkey lived in the place mentioned there; but he died (as I have been informed) of the plague in the year of our Lord 1665. His death was caused by venturing to perform an autopsy on a corpse that died of the plague (as Mr. Thomson the chemist had done before him, though Thomson lived many years afterward). Mr. Starkey’s adventure cost him his life. Nevertheless, the medicine truly made and prepared from mineral sulfur, called **native sulfur** <term>native sulfur</term> <note>original: "Sulphur Vive"; naturally occurring sulfur found in its pure mineral form</note>, can now be obtained from many chemists in and around London. Indeed, the difficulty in making it is not so great that you could not make it yourself if you wish, provided you wait for the time and opportunity to buy mineral sulfur (rather than common brimstone), as the mineral form is not always available.*

The process and design for the glass bell, and the method for making and purifying this **spirit** <term>spirit</term> <note>In alchemy, a "spirit" refers to a volatile substance or essence produced through distillation</note> from mineral sulfur—or native sulfur as it comes stone-like out of the earth—may be seen in the chemical works of Johann Hartmann and Oswald Croll called *Royal Chemistry* <note>original: "Crollius called Royal Chymistry", referring to the "Basilica Chymica"</note>, Moyse Charas’s *Royal Pharmacopoeia*, Nicaise Le Febure, Pierre Thibault, Nicolas Lemery, Christophe Glaser, Johann Schröder’s *Dispensatory*, and many others, to whom I refer you. *W. C. B.* <note>These initials likely refer to William Cooper, Bookseller, the editor and publisher of this collection.</note>

## New Remedies against Famine.

The first, principal, and most Christian advice <note>original: "Counsel"</note> that I can give in these threatening days of war <note>original: "Sword"</note> and famine, is to call upon the name of the great and mighty *Jehovah*, and of the gracious and merciful God of *Israel*, by sincere prayers from a *zealous* heart. 

We must pray that it may please Him to forget and forgive our many sins and transgressions, which have turned His favorable face <note>original: "Countenance"</note> away from us for so long. These sins have brought down so many clouds of wrath from heaven upon the fruits of the earth, so much so that the great hope of our *harvest* is already damaged and discouraged <note>original: "smitten and daunted"</note>. 

We also pray that it would please Him, out of His fatherly goodness and by whatever means seem best in His own eyes, to relieve our present needs and turn our scarcity <note>original: "penury"</note> into plenty. May He bless us with His bountiful hand so that we may all sing a full song of thanksgiving to Him, both for these new and undeserved favors, and for

<summary>The author argues that the primary remedy for famine and war is spiritual repentance and sincere prayer to God. He suggests that current crop failures are a result of divine wrath and calls for a return to God's favor to restore plenty.</summary>
<keywords>famine, prayer, repentance, harvest, Jehovah, divine providence, scarcity, seventeenth-century religion</keywords>

--- Page 172 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's list of recommendations for addressing famine, following his spiritual advice. It transitions from thanking God for military victories to addressing the practical administration of grain markets.</meta>

...that glorious victory recently obtained against our Catholic <note>original: "Popish"</note> adversaries, by the hand of those honorable commanders who have already begun to establish peace in our Commonwealth.

Secondly, I wish that all lower-ranking <note>original: "inferior"</note> officers in their respective positions would take more charitable and religious care in executing those orders which have recently been written and published—with serious and deliberate advice from the higher authorities—to supply our markets with all kinds of grain. For the enforcement <note>original: "Execution"</note> is the very life of the law; the letter of the law, even though set down by authority and supported by a most honorable assembly <note>original: "Chorus"</note>, gives some hope at first, but no full satisfaction in the end, unless the magistrate who enforces it joins hand-in-hand with those high directors for the common good of their distressed country.

Thirdly, although I have no authority to command, I cannot help but wish that the very food of the earth—which is the blessing of the Lord—should no longer be subject to this copyhold <term>copyhold: an ancient form of land tenure where the tenant held land at the "will of the lord," based on the customs of the local manor</term> and slavish control <note>original: "tenure"</note> of such base and unmerciful lords. These men, upon every rumor of foreign shortages, or every small-scale export <note>original: "petit-Transportation"</note>—indeed, even at the mere rumor of exportation—or upon fair weather,

<summary>The author calls for local officials to strictly enforce government orders regarding grain supplies and criticizes greedy landlords who manipulate food prices based on rumors of shortages or exports.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of economic exploitation during times of scarcity. He moves from discussing the manipulation of grain prices by "unmerciful lords" to a moral appeal for Christian abstinence and a warning to the wealthy against excess.</meta>

->### against Famine.<-

weather, or foul weather, or any weather if they choose <note>original: "lift"</note>, can make the same fineable <term>fineable: subject to a fee or "fine" paid by a tenant to a landlord</term> **at the will of the lord** <note>original: *ad voluntatem Domini*</note>, and set whatever price they wish upon the bushel. Is there no Court of Chancery, neither in heaven nor upon earth, to restrain <note>original: "bridle"</note> these covetous and unmerciful lords—yes, and to set a limit <note>original: "stint"</note> on them? So that, however it shall please God, the giver of all things, to cross us from the heavens, yet no inferior person should dare to exceed a certain price to be set down by authority, under the penalty of confiscating whatever grain he should so overcharge <note>original: "over rate"</note> his poor and needy neighbor for?

Fourthly, if ever abstinence were a true Christian virtue, then let it appear among us now; for why should the rich men feast, when the poor are ready to starve <note>original: "famish"</note>? Was there never but one **Dives** and one **Lazarus** upon the earth? <note>A reference to the biblical parable in Luke 16, where a rich man (often called Dives) feasts while the beggar Lazarus starves at his gate.</note> Or do we lack the wit, or will, or grace to apply a parable? # Now remedies

...as easily leave our lives as our lusts. Yet, if every rich man would spare just one meal a week and give the value <note>original: "estimate"</note> of it to the poor of the parish where he lives (**"It is never said too often, because it is never learned enough"** <note>original: *nunquam nimis dicitur, quod nunquam satis discitur*</note>), I say even this one meal would serve well to improve a whole week's food allowance <term>commons: a shared portion of food or the daily food ration</term> for a poor laborer <term>subscisor: a term for a lower-level tenant or poor worker, literally "one who cuts beneath"</term>.

My fifth and last petition is to move us toward Christian charity. If that Roman poet and orator, the wise <term>sententious: a style characterized by short, pithy moral sayings</term> **Seneca**, in the face of a private friend's danger and distress, could give this instruction: **"We ought to meet the needs of friends before they arise, rather than merely helping them once they are in need"** <note>original: *Quòd amicorum necessitati magis occurrendum, quàm succurrendum*</note>—meaning we should prevent a friend's hardship rather than just relieving it afterward—then what should be expected of us in a matter of such great weight and importance? 

This situation does not only affect the reputation <note>original: "credit"</note> of our Christian faith <note>original: "Profession"</note>, but also the lives and welfare of many thousands of our poor Christian brothers and sisters. Some of them are unable to work, many are unemployed <note>original: "without labour"</note>, and even those who do work can hardly support themselves by their labor. 

Indeed, if we would look more closely and pierce more deeply with a sharp eye into the threats and terrors of these times, even if religion could not produce any charity in us toward others, simple reason and social stability <note>original: "civil policy"</note> might influence us enough to act for our own sakes.

### *How to remove most of the rank and unpleasant taste of beans, peas, beechmast, chestnuts, acorns, vetches, and similar items.*

If this can be achieved to any significant degree, then I have no doubt that the volume and substance of our meal and flour will be greatly increased and multiplied—at the least for the poor man’s table. Here, then, is my own experience in the matter. Boil your beans, peas, beechmast <term>beechmast: the nuts of the beech tree, used historically as animal feed or emergency food</term>, etc., in clean water. If they are not yet pleasant enough, change your water again; by the second or third boiling, you shall find a remarkable change in taste, for the water has drawn out and absorbed the worst of their bitterness <note>original: "rankness"</note>. Then you must dry them. If you wish, you may also hull <term>hull: to remove the outer shell or husk of a seed or nut</term> them, following the method described later in the summary of *The Anchor of Famine* <note>original: "Anchora Famis"</note>. Alternatively, you may grind them with the hulls still on and make bread from them—either using the flour on its own or by adding a third or fourth part of wheat flour. ->*Certain strange and extraordinary ways for the relief of a prisoner, or other poor distressed creatures, when all hope of normal food <note>original: "Victuals"</note> is taken from them.*<-

## *How to make an excellent Bread from the Roots of Aaron, called Cuckoo-pint, or Starch Roots.*

The method for making it is described by a recent writer in this way: First, the large roots must be cleaned of all skin and dirt, and then cut into small, thin slices; the thinner you make them, the sooner they are prepared. Boil <note>original: "seeth"</note> them in boiling water as long as you find the water tastes hot and sharp <note>original: "biting"</note>, and until the roots begin to grow <note>original: "wax"</note> sweet. Then change your water and pour fresh water over them, and continue boiling until the water becomes sweet and the roots have lost all their bitterness <note>original: "acrimony; referring to the toxic, stinging chemicals in the raw plant"</note>. 

Then take them out and spread them out on canvas supported by frames; once they are dry, grind them with hand-mills. They produce a very white and pure flour <note>original: "meal"</note> which, either on its own or mixed with one-third wheat flour, makes a very beautiful and savory bread. This method makes good sense and seems likely to be true, for we find by daily experience that these roots produce as fine, if not a finer starch, than our wheat does. Therefore, it is to be hoped <note>original: "it were to be wished"</note> that good farming practices <note>original: "husbandry"</note> are used in the planting and cultivation <note>original: "multiplying"</note> of these roots,

### *Bread, and other food made of Pumpkins.* <note>original: "Pompions"</note>

This fruit, being both inexpensive and large, also makes a very savory bread if a little flour <note>original: "meal"</note> is mixed with it, providing food for a great number of people at a small cost. And if you add sugar and other seasonings <note>original: "sauce"</note> to it, it may even be considered a delicacy. ### *How to save much flour or meal that is lost in all our usual corn mills, whether they grind with wind or water.*

If I teach the miller how to grind his wheat so that neither the starch-maker (if I am not mistaken) shall have material to make his starch with—unless he grinds for himself in the ancient manner—nor the "brown baker" <term>a baker who produced lower-quality wholemeal or rye bread</term> have any bran to make horse-bread <term>a coarse bread made of bran, beans, and peas, intended for horses</term> with, I hope that my fault will be forgiven at this time. I believe it is much better to lack flour around our necks <note>This refers to the contemporary fashion of using starch to stiffen decorative neck ruffs</note> than in our bellies, and that horses should starve before their masters. <meta>This page continues the author's proposal for improving milling efficiency during times of food scarcity, specifically by re-grinding bran to extract more flour. It then introduces an alternative method for making laundry starch using wild roots instead of edible grain.</meta>

I hope this will be accepted without objection <note>original: "controlment"</note>. Let every mill that grinds grain <note>original: "corn"</note> also have a sifting mill <term>original: "boulting mill," a machine used to separate flour from bran</term> attached to it, so that the same power source <note>original: "mover"</note> can operate both. By shaking the sifter <note>original: "boulter"</note>, the machine will separate the bran from the flour. As soon as this bran is separated from the flour, it must be returned to the hopper along with the rest of the unground wheat. As quickly as you collect any bran, you must mix it with more grain. By this means, you will have much less bran and also more flour, even if you were to pass the flour through a fine sifter again afterward. It is common practice in Upper *Germany* to sift with these mills, but as far as I know or can learn, they do not grind their bran over again in the first mill.

### *How to make starch without using any grain.*

It is well known that the *Cuckoo-pint roots* <term>original: "Aaron-roots," the roots of the Arum maculatum plant, also known as Lords-and-Ladies</term> mentioned before will make a white and delicate starch. You must gather them in *March* or *April*, washing them clean and peeling away all the dirt or foul skins. Afterward, slice them into thin slices and leave them in clean, clear water, changing the water every 12 hours, for

...the space of four or five days, until they become exceedingly white and clean. Then crush <note>original: "stamp"</note> them and press them through a strainer with clean water. When the substance of the starch has settled at the bottom (which will happen in a few hours), very gently drain away all the clear water floating on the top. Place the remaining paste into flat earthen pans or clean tubs and expose it to the sun, which will draw up <note>original: "attract"</note> all the water and leave a hard cake at the bottom. 

But during the winter, when the sun is not strong enough for this purpose, place your stone pans or pewter basins (in which you have strained your starch) on top of a pot of scalding water; in this way, you can dry enough starch for your own use all year round. If you wish to harden the starch at no extra cost, place your pan on top of your beef pot <term>original: "bief pot," a large pot used for boiling meat for the household</term>; thus, you can make one fire perform several tasks at once. 

However, because these roots are not available in all places or at all times of the year, I have thought it helpful to provide the following recipe as a backup supply.

...ounces of this gum. First, wash it in clean tap water <note>original: "fair Conduit water"</note> in a stone basin, stirring it up and down with your hands to remove any dirt from it. Then cover your pan, and when all the gum has dissolved, strain the liquid through a clean, thin linen cloth. Store it in tightly sealed glass bottles until you need to use it. It will stay fresh <note>original: "sweet"</note> for at least three weeks after it is made. 

When you want to use this starch, if you want your ruffs <term>large, pleated frill collars popular in the 16th and 17th centuries</term> to have a pure and perfect white color, you must mix some laundry blue <note>original: "blew," a pigment used to counteract the natural yellowing of fabric</note> into the water. Stir it with your finger in a small bowl <note>original: "Porrenger"</note>, and before the blue pigment settles to the bottom, dip your ruff into it and immediately wring it out again. Then work the fabric until it is clear, and afterward "set" it (shaping the pleats) just as you do with common grain-based starch. 

I have found through experience that this method saves half the time lost in traditional starching. Because this starch is a thin liquid, fine fabrics like **lawn** and **cambric** <note>types of very fine, lightweight linen</note> will become clear quickly and with much less beating <note>the process of working starch into the fibers</note>. ### *A cheap drink for poor men when malt is extremely expensive.*

First, make a <term>decoction</term> <note>A decoction is an extract made by boiling plant material to dissolve its chemicals or flavors.</note> of 4 ounces of hops in nine gallons of water (which is the proportion some brewers use for certain types of drink). Once you have extracted their full strength and essence through boiling <note>original: "ebullition"</note>, set the liquid aside. Then, do the same with a small amount of wormwood in the same quantity of water as before. 

After you have spent as much time and fire on it as you did for the hops, taste each liquid individually. If you find the wormwood liquid to be more bitter than the first, then try again with a smaller amount of wormwood. Repeat this process until you have equally matched the bitterness of one to the other. Once this is done, you may safely use the rule of proportion to scale the recipe up to a barrel, and from there to a <term>tun</term> <note>A large cask or vat used for brewing or storing liquids, historically holding about 252 gallons.</note>, and eventually to a whole brewing batch. 

Do not be discouraged by the bitter taste of the wormwood at this stage. When these two are combined, they become a savory and wholesome drink for a person's body. This result can be achieved just as well with wormwood as with hops—and perhaps even with centaury, artichoke leaves, or *hepatic aloes* <note>original: "Aloes hepatique"; a type of medicinal aloe used as a bittering agent</note>, as some master craftsmen have confidently assured me. 

Although hops are usually used in drinks and wormwood only in medicine, some people might happen to be convinced that it is inappropriate for healthy people to consume a medicine continuously with their meals. However, let this be a sufficient answer to that objection: it is the dose alone that makes the difference in this case. 

For I can assure you from my own experience, and from the experience of one of the most skilled brewers in *London* (who is still living), that if you add a double or triple quantity of *English hops* to an ordinary <term>guile</term> <note>A "guile" or "gyle" is a single batch of beer brewed at one time.</note> of strong beer, you will find it serves as a sufficient preparation for your body for the most effective <term>purgation</term> <note>A medical treatment intended to "cleanse" the body by inducing bowel movements.</note> administered afterward. This is the same reason why *Venice turpentine* <note>A resin obtained from the larch tree, historically used for various ailments.</note>, when administered in a small dose, is given to strengthen the back and to stop the "running of the reins" <note>A historical term for urinary discharge or kidney issues.</note>. Yet, if it is taken in the quantity of an ounce at once, it will sufficiently purge various bodies...

### An Abstract of certain frugal Notes, or Observations in a time of Scarcity or Famine, concerning bread, drink, and meat, with some other circumstances belonging to the same, taken out of a Latin writer, who titled his book, *The Anchor of Hunger and Thirst* <note>original Latin: *Anchora famis & sitis*</note>.

1. First, to avoid all rot <note>original: "putrefaction"</note> in both bread and grain <note>original: "corn"</note>, it is very necessary that they be perfectly dried or gently parched, either in the sun or by the warmth of the air. If these two are unavailable, they should be dried in a suitable oven, or rather on a stone, but with such care that they do not burn or taste of scorching <note>original: "adustion"</note>.

2. After baking your bread, it is necessary to leave it in the oven, well-closed, for a reasonable amount of time while the heat is lessened by degrees. In this way, because the bread is thoroughly baked and allowed to cool on its own, it will satisfy a man's hunger twice as much <note>original: "in double proportion"</note> as it otherwise would.

<meta>catchword: 3 Each</meta>

<summary>This page introduces a set of practical tips for preserving food during a famine, specifically focusing on the proper drying of grain and the efficient baking of bread to maximize its nutritional value.</summary>

3. Every type of bread, if lightly toasted over coals and then soaked <note>original: "sopped"</note> in wine, will be extremely filling. A breakfast like this, eaten in the morning, is enough to sustain someone for the entire day.

4. Flour <note>original: "meal"</note> made from parched grain <note>original: "corn"</note> is very filling.

5. Bread can be made from *rice*, *Indian millet*, or *maize* <note>original: "Turkish wheat"</note>, either by boiling <note>original: "decocting"</note> the whole grain in water to create a *porridge* <note>original: "pulteis"</note> and then baking it, or by grinding it into flour <note>original: "meal"</note>. However, grinding it into flour creates a better-looking bread. *In my opinion, this would work just as well with our common wheat.*

6. All types of legumes <note>original: "pulse"</note>, such as lentils, vetches, beans, and similar crops, will produce better flour and bread if they are first rubbed in lye <note>original: "lee," an alkaline solution used in food processing</note>, hulled <note>meaning: the outer skins removed</note>, and then ground.

7. Paste or dough is quickly baked on thin plates made of iron or brass.

8. Those who travel by express relay <note>original: "ride Post"</note> are often satisfied to both bake their bread and roast their meat under their saddles <note>the friction and body heat from the horse were traditionally said to cook thin pieces of food</note>. However, I believe our climate would be too cold for this to work here.

9 People must be introduced gradually, and not too suddenly, to these artificial diets instead of their usual and natural food and drink.

10 A porridge <note>original: "pulteis"</note> or stew <note>original: "hochpot"</note>—made of flour or meal boiled <note>original: "sodden"</note> with apples, pears, plums, and similar fruit; or made from some bread and water, or the broth of meat <note>original: "flesh"</note> that has been toasted in the smoke, or with milk well boiled together—fills the stomach more than three times as much as dry bread eaten alone, especially if it is boiled until it reaches a stiff consistency.

11 Similar preparations also go much further in satisfying hungry stomachs <note>original: "maws"</note> when they are made from biscuits <note>original: "biskets," referring to hard-tack or twice-baked bread</note>, or dry, hard, or stale grated bread. By this method, one loaf will go as far as two fresh loaves.

12 All types of good enriched bread <note>original: "cakebread"</note> or spiced bread <note>original: "spicebread"</note>, when soaked for a suitable amount of time in clean <note>original: "fair"</note> water, will turn the water into a very pleasant and wholesome drink; the bread itself remains very healthy to eat afterward.

13 Grind <note>original: "pound"</note> your pepper, ginger, and similar spices, and after soaking them in water, place them in a well-covered container over a gentle fire. Then, mix your dough <note>original: "paste"</note> using the resulting liquid <note>original: "imbibition or decoction," referring to the water that has absorbed the spice flavors through boiling</note>. By this method, your spices will go much further.

<summary>This page offers advice on stretching food supplies during a famine by making dense porridges and stews, utilizing stale bread, and extracting maximum flavor from spices.</summary>

Here you may experiment <note>original: "practise"</note> with these plants, which are pungent <note>original: "hot"</note> and wholesome as well: such as wild cresses (otherwise called pepperwort), galingale <note>a root similar to ginger</note>, thyme, orache <note>original: "Orrace"; a leafy vegetable similar to spinach</note>, hyssop <note>original: "Isop"</note>, winter savory, pennyroyal, and similar herbs instead of spices.

15 The smell or scent of bread (*I think the author means that which is fresh and hot from the oven*) greatly nourishes the body and refreshes the spirit. Some recommend the "spirits of bread" extracted through distillation as a most excellent <note>original: "soveraign"</note> remedy <note>original: "preservative"</note> for tuberculosis <note>original: "the Consumption"</note> and other wasting <note>original: "pining"</note> diseases.

16 If any of these artificial foods or drinks happen to be unappealing <note>original: "offend"</note> in color, taste, or flavor, they may be improved with honey, sugar, saffron, wine, aniseed <note>original: "Annis-seeds"</note>, coriander seeds, sweet fennel, cinnamon, and the like.

# New Remedies

17 In times of necessity, even green grain <note>original: "corn"; in this period, "corn" referred to any cereal grain like wheat or barley, not specifically maize</note> eaten as it grows, or slightly toasted or dried by the fire, or soaked or boiled in wine or water, provides a reasonable amount of nourishment.

18 Distilled oat water warms the stomach so effectively that it overcomes the senses. *It is well known that many brew a very strong and powerful drink with malted oats. This could be very profitable for all our English brewers (if a sufficient supply could be found) during a shortage <note>original: "dearth"</note> of wheat and barley, especially if the oats are properly balanced, or rather tempered slightly with hops to improve their taste. Those who have experimented and profited from this privately can best attest to it, while others have thoughtlessly continued their usual, expensive <note>original: "chargeable"</note> brewing methods.*

19 Birch tree sap <note>original: "liquor"</note> is both wholesome and flavorful, and deserves to be recommended for its qualities.

20 An excellent extract can be made from ale, which you might call either a spirit or a "quintessence" <note>an alchemical term for the purest essence or "fifth element" of a substance</note>. Even in a small dose, it is far superior to all preparations involving tartar, sulfur, or mercury <note>these were the three primary substances in Paracelsian medicine</note>. *If the author is referring to a complex alchemical <note>original: "Philosophical"</note> process, it will be too complicated <note>original: "curious"</note> and expensive for ordinary people. However, if he only means a well-purified <note>original: "rectified"</note> spirit <note>original: "Aquavitæ"; Latin for "water of life"</note>, or the removal of the watery <note>original: "Phlegmatick"</note>...*

part of a thick body, I cannot see how we shall raise any store or quantity of matter to furnish the subject which we have in hand. If he means this physically <note>original: "physically"; here meaning in terms of natural science or medicine</note>, we will reserve the strict examination of it until a more fitting occasion is offered.

21 The flour <note>original: "meal"</note> of such grain <note>original: "corn"; in this period, "corn" referred to any cereal grain like wheat or barley</note> as is ground in the month of August is recorded among the writers of best credit to keep and last best all the year after.

22 Such bread as is made up of the flour of dry beans is very high in nourishment, and its taste may be improved <note>original: "corrected"</note> by the addition of cumin seed. It is also a common practice in Germany to make a drink from beans. Our English brewers also find good use for them among other grains <note>original: "Corn"</note> in a small proportion, though they take special care not to overload <note>original: "surcharge"</note> the rest of their brewing grain <note>original: "Beer Corn"</note> with too great a quantity of beans, lest they should give a bad flavor or aftertaste <note>original: "smack or farewel"</note> to their beer. But I am truly persuaded that if either beans or peas were skillfully processed <note>original: "artificially handled"; "art" in this context refers to technical skill or specialized methods</note> according to the manner described before, they would not only prove useful—and in a large capacity—for beer alone, but also for the making of wholesome, sweet, and delicious bread.

23 From vetches <note>original: "Vetches"; a type of legume often used for livestock fodder, but here suggested for human use</note> that have first been hulled, and from the herb *Aphace* <note>original: "Aphace"; an old botanical name here linked to the dandelion family</note>—which has various names and is called Dandelion, Priest's crown, Swine's snout, Monk's head, Dog's teeth, or common Chicory—a bread may be made, provided it is mixed with a proper proportion of other usual flour <note>original: "meal"</note>. This mixture yields a very beautiful and savory flour <note>original: "Flower"</note>, as the author testifies from his own experience. The flavor may be improved <note>original: "corrected"</note> with aniseed, fennel seed, coriander seed, etc.

24 Both bread and drink may also be made from lentils.

25 Bread may be made of foxtail millet <note>original: "Pannick"</note>, as well as common millet, whose seed—even in a small quantity—expands greatly <note>original: "arise greatly"</note> in both bulk and substance.

26 A solid and wholesome bread may be made of wheat starch. *But such bread, by reason of its price, will have no fit place here—unless every private man provides his own supply.*

27 A decoction <note>original: "decoction"; a liquid made by boiling medicinal or herbal substances</note> of aniseed, fennel seed, caraway seed, and such like, in either wine or water, is a most wholesome drink. *To this may also be added a decoction of licorice <note>original: "Licoras"</note> with aniseeds together in clean <note>original: "fair"</note> water in a proper proportion.*

# Against Famine

28 From beechmast <note>original: "Beechmast"; the small nuts of the beech tree, once a common forest food</note>, acorns, and the bark or scrapings <note>original: "raping"</note> of wholesome trees, a suitable drink may be made.

29 Mushrooms will grow abundantly if you slit the bark of the black and white poplar and bury them in well-fertilized <note>original: "dunged"</note> furrows. Likewise, if the white poplar is cut off close to the ground and watered with warm water well-seasoned with leaven <note>original: "Leaven"; a fermented dough used as a starter or yeast</note>, it will bring forth the most pleasant and delicate mushrooms in four days' time. *When prepared in their various ways, these are considered among the most invigorating <note>original: "lusty"</note> and stimulating meats by the Italians.*

30 A good bread may be made of the rape or navew <note>original: "Rape, or Navew"; root vegetables in the brassica family, similar to turnips or rutabagas</note>, by first scorching them, then boiling <note>original: "sodden"</note> them, and finally baking them.

31 A bread may be made of the powdered or ground leaves of the pear tree, apple tree, beech, and oak; a drink may also be made in the same way.

32 Dough <note>original: "Dow"</note> may be kneaded with *wine*, *vinegar*, or *ale* if you wish to make it hot and hearty. *But I believe that the new must <note>original: "Muſt"; freshly pressed, unfermented grape juice</note> of wine, or the best wort <note>original: "Wort"; the liquid extracted from the mashing process during brewing</note> of ale or beer, is much better. We may well suspect—or rather be certain—that the whole spirit of the wine or ale will evaporate <note>original: "fly away"</note> during baking because it had already fully fermented <note>original: "wrought itself into a body"</note>. In contrast, in wort that has not yet been processed <note>original: "come to workmanship"</note>, the heat or spirit still lies hidden and contained within it.*

# *New Remedies*

33 A "drunken bread" <note>original: "drunken bread"; bread prepared with alcohol instead of water</note> can be made using spirits of wine <note>original: "spirit of Wine"; distilled alcohol or brandy</note> and flour. *However, I believe that ordinary brandy <note>original: "Aqua-composita"; a general term for distilled herbal spirits or brandy</note> would prove too expensive <note>original: "over chargeable"</note>.*

34 A paste consisting of meal and olive oil—or oil from other fruits or seeds mixed together—can be made into bread.

35 Mizaldus <note>Antoine Mizauld (1510–1578) was a French physician and astrologer who wrote extensively on "natural secrets" and medicine</note> reports of a certain traveler who, undertaking a long journey, sustained himself with one pound of violet oil and soft grease <note>fat or lard</note> mixed together; with this, he survived for ten days. A similar effect has also been found using almond oil mixed with cow fat, due to its thick consistency <note>original: "clamminess"</note>.

36 Bread made with eggs is both wholesome and more filling than ordinary bread, especially if it is kneaded with the yeast <note>original: "yiest"</note> from the strongest beer or ale.

37 The eggs to be most carefully gathered and kept are those laid starting from the new moon in August; others instead recommend the waning moon <note>original: "Wain"</note> and the times of both solstices <note>original: "Sunsteads"</note>. New-laid eggs will keep for a long time in dry chaff or bran.

38 An excellent bread can be made with milk, either leavened or unleavened; it provides exceptional nourishment even when eaten in a

## A Petition to the Gracious Reader.

Here I have thought it right (*Gentle Reader*) to ask this much favor from you: since my new fuel of *coalballs* <note>original: "Coleballs"; a manufactured fuel made of coal dust mixed with binders like clay or sawdust, designed to burn longer and cleaner</note>, along with a few other *inventions* first mentioned in my *Apology* <note>original: "Apology"; in this context, a formal written defense or explanation of his earlier work</note>, are still awaiting certain royal *approvals* <note>original: "courtly favours"</note>. Because of this, they cannot be released for the public service of this *country* as quickly as I would like. 

I ask that for a little while (which I hope is now drawing to an *end* <note>original: "period"</note>), you would view them with a favorable *attitude* <term>conceit</term> and kind *opinion*. Do not pay attention to the *criticisms* <note>original: "censures"</note> of those *ignorant* or *malicious people* of our age. These people, assuming they know the *ingredients* <term>simples</term> of my *fuel*, may unfortunately stray into base and offensive topics. By doing so, they labor to discredit my secret, whose *formula* <note>original: "composition"</note> they could never actually grasp; nor, even if they had the *details*, would they be able to combine and join them with their clumsy *craftsmanship* <note>original: "left handed Workmanship"</note>.

And for the better satisfaction of my well-wishing friends, and the full proof against my undeserving enemies, I would have them understand that since the aforementioned secrets have not only been seen and approved, but at this present time 

<meta>catchword: are</meta>

<summary>In this preface, the author asks the reader for patience and a fair trial of his new inventions, particularly a manufactured fuel called "coalballs," while he awaits official government approval. He defends his work against critics and amateur imitators who claim to understand his secret formulas but lack the skill to replicate them.</summary>
<keywords>coalballs, inventions, fuel technology, patent approval, industrial secrets, craftsmanship, social critics</keywords>

<header>To the Courteous Reader.</header>

*are also supported by those who are truly **Honorable** in their positions: so that from now on they will scorn the malice of both* **venomous tongues** <note>original: "Viperous Tongues"</note>, *as well as* **slanderous pens**, *should anyone happen to be so extremely or desperately mad as to take it upon himself to argue against that project—a project for which he can find neither a* **middle ground** <note>original: *medium*; in formal logic, the "middle term" that connects two premises</note> *nor* **common terms** <note>original: *communes terminos*</note>. *It is therefore impossible to reach a conclusion* **logically** <note>original: *Syllogisticè*</note> *against it, unless he does so in* **Bocardo** <note>original: *si non in Bocardo*; a clever double-entendre. In formal logic, "Bocardo" is a specific, valid form of syllogism. However, "Bocardo" was also the name of a famous prison in Oxford. The author is suggesting that his critics' arguments are so illogical that the only "conclusion" they deserve is to be thrown in jail.</note>.

<summary>This title page introduces an alchemical or hermetic treatise that uses the legend of Queen Semiramis's tomb as a metaphor for hidden spiritual and philosophical knowledge. It warns that such secrets are available only to the wise and virtuous, rather than the greedy.</summary>

### To the most Noble and most Famous, S.R. J., diligent students of Nature, and my most honored Patrons.

Not only in former times, but also in this current aging state of the world <note>original: "this old Age of the World"</note>, nothing has been more deeply buried, nor until now more desired by everyone—especially by the lovers of the Art <note>original: "lovers of Art"; in this context, the "Art" refers to alchemy or the Hermetic sciences</note>—than the knowledge of that great Mystery of the Philosophers, which is commonly called the <term>Philosopher's Stone</term> <note>The legendary alchemical substance believed to be capable of turning base metals into gold and granting eternal life.</note>. Therefore, by preserving the inscription of the monument, we are raising this knowledge (which was buried and hidden under the pretense of the tomb of the most wise Semiramis, the former Queen of Babylon <note>Semiramis was a legendary Queen of Babylon. According to historical myth, her tomb carried a deceptive inscription that promised wealth but was intended to teach a lesson to the greedy.</note>) out of its grave, and...

<sig>A 2</sig>

<summary>In this dedication, the author announces the rediscovery of the "Philosopher's Stone," comparing the unearthing of this hidden alchemical knowledge to opening the legendary tomb of Queen Semiramis of Babylon.</summary>
<keywords>Philosopher's Stone, Semiramis, alchemy, Babylon, Hermetic philosophy, secrets of nature</keywords>

--- Page 222 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to the author's patrons, further developing the metaphor of the "tomb" of alchemical knowledge and the difficulty of truly understanding it.</meta>

# The Epistle

...present it to the view of the learned. If a wise man opens it (not an ambitious, greedy *Cyrus* <note>original: "covetous Cyrus"; Cyrus the Great, King of Persia, was said in legend to have opened the tomb of Semiramis seeking wealth, only to find a reprimand for his greed.</note>), he will find to his satisfaction *Royal* and inexhaustible *treasures*. I may also add constant *health*—the two things, you will agree, that are the main pillars of a happy life.

But perhaps some people of no small cleverness will imagine that this *tomb* may be opened by "women's work" or "children's play" <note>In alchemy, "women's work" (*opus mulierum*) and "children's play" (*ludus puerorum*) were common terms for the simpler or more mechanical stages of the alchemical process.</note>. However, let them know that this *knowledge* is a very deep *river*. In it, the *Lamb* wades—meaning the upright person profits—and the *Elephant* swims—meaning the most *learned* fluctuate, or are even drowned, in their opinions, errors, and doubts. While one person remains doubtful about the true subject of the <term>Great Work</term> <note>The "Great Work" (*Magnum Opus*) is the alchemical process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note>, or is even a total stranger to it, another is very anxious...

<summary>The author warns that alchemical knowledge is a "deep river" where the simple find success but the overly intellectual often lose their way, offering both wealth and health to the wise seeker.</summary>
<keywords>Cyrus the Great, Great Work, alchemical metaphors, wisdom vs greed, treasures of nature, health</keywords>

--- Page 223 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's dedication ("The Epistle"), focusing on the frustrations and failures of misguided alchemical seekers and the author's charitable decision to reveal the true path.</meta>

<header>Dedicatory.</header>

...in acquiring the *philosophical Mercury* <note>original: "sophical Mercury"</note>, wearies himself with unsuccessful labors, not knowing what it is or what form it takes. Another tortures himself with vain anxiety to know what the *Philosopher's Fire* is, what the magical *elements* are, or the *key*—the dissolving *solvent* <term>menstruum</term> <note>original: "Menstruum"; in alchemy, this refers to a powerful solvent capable of dissolving solids into their primary matter.</note>. He wonders where it should be drawn from: is it sweet or corrosive? Cold or fiery?

It has often troubled me to see such men so concerned and laboring in vain over those things, in which they could ultimately find nothing but emptiness and mental distress. Therefore, out of pity for them and moved by *charity*, I freely share (though many will accuse me of breaking the vow of silence) the *light* mercifully granted to me, so that they may use it as a *key* to the *sanctuary* of that *sacred knowledge*.

<sig>A 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: But</meta>

<summary>The author describes the common failures of alchemists who struggle to identify the correct materials and methods, prompting him to charitably reveal the "Light" or "Key" to true alchemical knowledge.</summary>
<keywords>philosophical Mercury, Philosopher's Fire, alchemical elements, solvent, menstruum, charity, secrecy vs revelation, sacred knowledge</keywords>

# The Epistle.

But your careful *industry* and tireless *curiosity*—you being most excellent in the exalted secrets <note>original: "Arcana's"; referring to hidden or mysterious knowledge, often in a scientific or alchemical context.</note> and works of many-skilled <note>original: "Polydædalus"; a reference to Daedalus, the mythological Greek craftsman, here used to describe the intricate and complex craftsmanship of nature.</note> nature—having invited all the learned men of the world through your most persuasive encouragements to share their most secret findings, you enjoin and oblige me to make you (the true sons of every kind of *knowledge*) heirs of this work. In my judgment, this is the most precise and ingenious work of all nature. But do not ask who I am. I am a man who makes it his study to benefit others; I am your friend and an admirer of your virtues, known to many, at least by name.

Farewell, therefore, you students of nature and high priests of art, the lofty stars of *Germany*. May God be with you and strengthen your works and thoughts with His power, so that they may be highly advantageous to the entire community of scholars <note>original: "Commonwealth of Learning"</note>, for the increase of the public good and the immortal glory of your own names.

From my study,  
*January 1, 1674.*

<summary>The author concludes his dedication by offering his work to the learned scholars of Germany, whom he praises for their curiosity and dedication to uncovering nature's secrets.</summary>
<keywords>Arcana, Polydædalus nature, Commonwealth of Learning, Germany, scientific community, anonymity, public good</keywords>

### THE
# Tomb of SEMIRAMIS
## Hermetically Sealed.
<note>The "Tomb of Semiramis" refers to a legendary story about the Queen of Babylon, who allegedly left a tomb with an inscription promising riches to whoever opened it, only for the opener to find a warning against greed. In alchemy, it is a metaphor for hidden wisdom that requires the right spirit to uncover. "Hermetically" refers to Hermes Trismegistus, the mythical founder of alchemy.</note>

---

### CHAPTER I.
### *Concerning the Physical Subject of the Philosopher's Stone.*

The fear of the Lord is the beginning of our work, and its end is charity and the love of our neighbor. Entering, therefore, into such a divine work with the assistance of our good God, we must first inquire what its subject <note>original: "subject"; in an alchemical context, this refers to the *materia prima* or the starting material required to create the Philosopher's Stone.</note> is. For just as a plowman prepares his ground for harvest in vain unless he is certain of his seed, so too does a person prepare the chemical <note>original: "Chymical"; at this time, "chemistry" and "alchemy" were often used interchangeably to describe the study of matter and its transformations.</note> ground without any reward if he...

<summary>The author begins his alchemical treatise by emphasizing that the work must be rooted in piety and charity, and argues that identifying the correct starting material (the "seed") is the essential first step of the process.</summary>
<keywords>Semiramis, Hermetic, Philosopher's Stone, Alchemy, Materia Prima, chemical ground, piety, Chymical</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "subject" or starting material of the Philosopher's Stone. The previous page ended with an analogy about a plowman needing the correct seed before he can harvest.</meta>

# The Tomb of Semiramis
<note>original: "Tumba Semiramidis"</note>

...does not know what he sows there. In this regard, many people today perplex themselves and are hurried into different opinions.

But this is not the place to discuss all these things. Some seek the subject in the **Animal Kingdom** in blood, sperm, sweat, urine, hair, dung, eggs, serpents, toads, spiders, and so on. Others are employed with great diligence in the **Vegetable Kingdom**, especially in wine, for the unprofitable *magistery* <note>original: "Magistery"; in alchemy, this refers to the master-process of transformation or the finished medicinal product.</note>.

For though it is manifest to us that the supreme medicine for our health may be obtained in either kingdom—and indeed in man (especially in his heart), as also in wine—for just as gold contains the virtues of all minerals, so do these two comprehend the powers of all animals and vegetables as if contracted into one. Yet, that the "Great Work" of philosophers could be made from them was never in the thought of any *adept* <note>An "adept" is someone who has mastered the secrets of alchemy and successfully created the Philosopher's Stone.</note>; it is therefore requisite to seek it in the **Mineral Kingdom**. 

But there is also a great company of dissenters here, so that we have need of an *Oedipus* <note>In Greek mythology, Oedipus was the hero who solved the riddle of the Sphinx; the author uses this as a metaphor for someone capable of solving the complex riddles of alchemy.</note>. For there are some who think to extract it out of the "middle minerals," as they call them—namely salt, nitre, alum, and such others—but all in vain, because they have in them no *argent-vive* <note>original: "Argent vive"; literally "living silver," the archaic term for mercury or quicksilver, which alchemists believed was the essential metallic soul.</note> into which they may be resolved. Even we, in our primitive ignorance, were also in- <note>The text continues on the next page with the word "involved."</note>

<summary>The author argues that the Philosopher's Stone must be sought in the Mineral Kingdom rather than the Animal or Vegetable Kingdoms, and warns that "middle minerals" like salt or alum are unsuitable because they lack mercury.</summary>
<keywords>Animal Kingdom, Vegetable Kingdom, Mineral Kingdom, Magistery, Adept, Oedipus, Salt, Nitre, Alum, Argent-vive, Mercury</keywords>

# The Tomb of Semiramis
<note>original: "Tumba Semiramidis"</note>

...involved. It remains, therefore, to be assumed that metals are the physical subject of our blessed Stone. But even here, the matter remains in suspense, because some metals are perfect and others are imperfect.

But ultimately, we say that all melted <note>original: "fused"</note> metals—but especially those that are not melted, even if imperfect—may become the subject of the Stone. This is achieved through the deep purification <note>original: "intimate depuration"</note> of their original contamination <note>original: "pollution"</note>, though this is very difficult and, to outward appearances, seems hardly possible. Regarding this, *Flamel* <note>Nicolas Flamel (c. 1330–1418) was a famous French scribe and manuscript-seller who was posthumously reputed to have discovered the Philosopher's Stone.</note> says that some have worked with tin <note>original: "Jupiter"</note> and others with lead <note>original: "Saturn"</note>, but he says, "I have worked and found it out in gold <note>original: "Sol"</note>." And in the *Exercises on the Assembly of the Philosophers* <note>original: "Exercit. ad Turbam"; refers to the *Turba Philosophorum*, one of the oldest Latin alchemical texts.</note> it is written, *That all metals, clean and unclean, are internally gold <note>original: "Sol"</note>, silver <note>original: "Luna"</note>, and mercury, but there is one true gold <note>original: "Sol"</note> which is drawn from them.*

And the author of *The Secret Work of the Hermetic Philosophy*, Canon 16, says: *He who seeks the art of multiplying and perfecting imperfect metals by any means other than the nature of metals deviates from the truth; for metals must be expected from metals, just as the species of man comes from man, and beast from beast.*

And in Canon 18, he continues: *Perfect bodies are endowed with a more perfect seed. Therefore, under the hard shell of the perfect metals lies the perfect seed; whoever knows how to extract it by philosophical dissolution <note>original: "Resolution"</note> has entered onto the Royal Path.* So also

<summary>The author argues that metals are the true source of the Philosopher's Stone, noting that while some alchemists work with tin or lead, the "perfect seed" is ultimately found within gold and silver.</summary>

-> Also, the anonymous *Philalethes* <note>Eirenaeus Philalethes was a famous 17th-century alchemist, often identified as the American George Starkey.</note> writes in his *Open Entrance to the Secret Palace of the King* <note>original: "Introduction into the King’s Sacred Palace"; Latin: *Introitus apertus ad occlusum regis palatium*</note>, Chapter 19, concerning the progress of the work in the first forty days: <-

"Indeed, gold exists in all metals—even common ones—but it is more closely present in gold and silver. However (as the same adept <note>A master or successful practitioner of alchemy.</note> rightly says), there is yet one substance in the metallic kingdom of remarkable origin in which our 'gold' is more closely present than in common gold and silver—if you seek it at the moment of its creation <note>original: "hour of its nativity"</note>. This substance melts in our Mercury like ice in warm water," and so forth.

But leaving aside these more "imperfect" metals for now, we declare those two great and more perfect luminaries, gold <note>original: "Sol," meaning the Sun</note> and silver <note>original: "Luna," meaning the Moon</note>, to be the physical subject of the Stone. This is the path that a great number of philosophers have followed and by which they reached their desired goal. Augurello <note>Giovanni Aurelio Augurello (c. 1441–1524), an Italian poet and alchemist.</note> shows this same thing in the second book of his *Gold-Making* <note>original: "Chrysop."; Latin: *Chrysopoeia*</note> when he says:

> Take a metal that is pure and purged of all its waste <note>original: "dross"</note>. Its spirit withdraws into its secret part; and being pressed down with a great weight, it lives privately and desires to be released from its bonds and to be sent out of prison to Heaven, once it is spread into thin plates.

He says the same in *Gold-Making*, Book 1:

> Do not seek the principles of gold anywhere else, for the seed of gold is within gold itself; though it is shut up tight, it retreats further within and must be sought by us with

# The Tomb of Semiramis
<note>original: "Tumba Semiramidis"</note>

...tedious labor. And concerning the dignity of both the luminaries <note>The "luminaries" refer to the sun (gold) and the moon (silver).</note>, Ramon Llull <note>original: "Lully"; a 13th-century philosopher and major figure in alchemical tradition.</note>, that star of spagyric <term>alchemy involving the separation and recombination of natural elements</term> philosophy, says in his book *The Powers of Medicines* <note>original: "P. M. 28," likely referring to *Potestate Medicinarum*</note>:

*Two metals are more pure than the rest, namely, Gold and Silver. Without these, the Work cannot be begun or finished, because in them is the purest substance of Sulphur, perfectly purified by the ingenuity of Nature. Out of these two bodies, prepared with their Sulphur or Arsenic <note>In alchemy, "arsenic" refers to a volatile principle or "spirit," not just the modern chemical element.</note>, our Medicine may be extracted, and it cannot be obtained without them.*

And the text *The Sound of the Trumpet* <note>original: "Clangor Buccinæ," a well-known 16th-century alchemical treatise.</note> says:

*You must operate prudently and precisely, because neither Gold <note>original: "Sol"</note> nor Silver <note>original: "Luna"</note> can function without ferment <term>an agent that triggers a transformation, like yeast in bread</term>. No other seed or ferment is proper or useful except Gold for the "red" work and Silver for the "white" work. These bodies must first be refined <note>original: "subtiliated"</note> under specific weight and then "sown" so that they may rot and be corrupted. In this process, one form is destroyed and another, more noble form is taken on; and this is done by the means of our Water alone.*

From this, a certain anonymous author in his *Answer* excellently concludes:

*As Fire is the source of Fire, so Gold is the source of Gold. As the Cause is, so is the Effect; as the Father is, so is the Son; as the Seed is, so is the Fruit. Man generates Man, and a Lion generates a Lion.*

But you will say: "The Philosophers affirm that the matter ought to be something so common that the Poor as well as the Rich may obtain it."

However (as that same *Adept* <term>a master who has successfully created the Philosopher's Stone</term> rightly says), there is yet one thing in the Metallic Kingdom of a remarkable origin in which our Gold is nearer than in common Gold and Silver. If you seek it at the hour of its birth <note>original: "nativity"</note>, it melts in our *Mercury* <note>In alchemy, "Mercury" usually refers to a specialized "philosophical" solvent rather than the common metal.</note> like ice in warm water," etc.

# The Tomb of Semiramis <note>original: "Tumba Semiramidis"</note>

### Page 12

From this comes the saying: *God has granted this treasure to be sought by all people; nor does he deny that great Good to anyone, except to those who make themselves unworthy through the corrupt desires <note>original: "depraved affections"</note> of their hearts.* And Geber <note>Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–815), a Persian polymath often considered the father of chemistry and a legendary figure in alchemy.</note> says, *You should not waste your resources because of a low price: if you understand the principles of Art which we will teach you, you will achieve the complete Mastery <note>original: "Magistery," referring to the successful creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>. For if the starting material were gold, or any such expensive thing, the poor would be forced to give up on this glorious Work.* Furthermore, since an alchemist <note>original: "Artist"</note> might often make a mistake, a poor person would be unable to restart the Work after an error occurred—which is absolutely necessary if there is no other solution. And the text *The Lily of Alchemy* <note>original: "Lilium," likely referring to the "Lilium Alchymiae," a famous alchemical treatise.</note> says, *This Stone is sold openly at the cheapest rate; if the sellers knew its value, they would keep it for themselves and would never sell it.* And another anonymous author says, *Our expenses do not exceed the price of two florins <note>A florin was a common gold or silver coin used in Europe.</note>.* Arnoldus <note>Arnold of Villanova (c. 1240–1311), a famous physician and alchemist.</note> confirms this, saying: *Hold fast, because the cost of our most noble Art does not exceed the price of two pieces of gold in its purchase <note>original: "emption"</note>—that is, in its performance.* And Geber says, *If you lose your money during these operations, do not speak ill of us, but blame your own lack of wisdom; for our Art requires no great expense.*

To this we answer: we never denied that, in addition to gold and silver, there is also another subject of meaner <note>The text ends mid-word; the next page continues with "er".</note>...

<summary>This page argues that alchemy is accessible to the poor because the true "Stone" or starting material is inexpensive and common, despite the previous page's emphasis on gold and silver. It cites several famous alchemists to reassure the reader that the "Great Work" does not require massive financial investment.</summary>

--- Page 233 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the low cost of alchemical materials, specifically addressing the paradox of using gold and silver in the Work. It clarifies that while common metals are "dead," the "Philosophers' Gold" is a revived, seminal version used to create the final tincture.</meta>

# The Tomb of Semiramis <note>original: "Tumba Semiramidis"</note>

<header>The Tomb of Semiramis.</header>

...er value, where we did not exclude imperfect metals, as we mentioned before from an anonymous philosopher in these words: "*There is yet one thing in the metallic kingdom of a wonderful origin, etc.*" although many philosophers would have this cheap price <note>original: "vile price"</note> to be understood as referring to our dissolving solvent <term>menstruum: a solvent used in alchemy to dissolve solid materials, often compared to a biological fluid</term>.

Moreover, you might argue based on the eleventh treatise of Sendivogius <note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636), a famous Polish alchemist and pioneer of chemistry.</note> that "In your operations, do not take common gold and silver, for these are dead things."

We answer by agreeing that the Stone <note>The Philosopher's Stone.</note> is not made of common gold and silver in their usual state while they are "dead." But when they are revived <note>original: "resuscitated"</note> and reduced into their primary seminal nature—made like the "Philosophers' Gold"—then they not only express their "seed" but also serve as a ferment <term>ferment: a substance that triggers a transformation or "ripening" in the alchemical process, much like yeast in baking</term>. A certain philosopher confirms this, saying: "*Neither the ancient nor the modern philosophers have ever made anything but gold from gold, and silver from silver; yet that was not common gold or silver.*"

From this it appears that the Philosophers' Gold is not common gold, neither in color nor in substance; rather, what is extracted from them is the white and red tincture <term>tincture: in alchemy, a potent substance or "elixir" capable of transmuting metals or perfecting the human body</term>.

->CHAPTER<-

<summary>This page addresses the alchemical paradox of using gold to make gold, explaining that while "common" gold is dead and useless, "Philosophers' Gold" is a revived version that acts as a seed or ferment for the Work. It clarifies that the true goal is the extraction of a "tincture" rather than the use of raw, unrefined bullion.</summary>
<keywords>Sendivogius, Philosophers' Gold, Menstruum, Ferment, Tincture, Metallic Kingdom, Resuscitation, Alchemical Theory</keywords>

# The Tomb of Semiramis <note>original: "Tumba Semiramidis"</note>

<header>The Tomb of Semiramis.</header>

## CHAPTER II.
# What the *Physical* or *Philosophers'* *Gold* is.

The Philosophers' Gold or Silver is a metallic body resolved into its ultimate state <note>original: "last matter"</note>—namely, into Mercury, which is the "first matter" of the Stone. This is proven as follows: everything originates from that into which it can be broken down; since all metals can be reduced into quicksilver <note>original: "Argent vive," from the Latin *argentum vivum*</note>, therefore <note>original: "ergo"</note> they were originally quicksilver.

For, according to the common opinions of philosophers, that which the wise seek is found in Mercury. Furthermore, Mercury is the root <note>original: "radix"</note> in alchemy, because all metals come from it, exist through it, and are contained within it. And **Theophrastus** <note>Theophrastus von Hohenheim, better known as Paracelsus (1493–1541), a foundational figure in the transition from alchemy to modern chemistry.</note>—that most profound sea of **spagyric philosophy** <term>spagyric philosophy: a branch of alchemy focused on the separation and recombination of natural elements, especially to create medicines</term>—speaks as follows concerning the primary matter of metals:

> *To extract Mercury from metallic bodies is nothing other than to resolve or reduce them into their first matter—that is, running Mercury. It is just as it was in the center of the Earth before the formation of metals: namely, a moist and viscous vapor. This is the Philosophers' Gold or Silver, containing within it invisibly the "Mercury" and "Sulphur of Nature"—the principles of all metals. This Mercury possesses inexpressible power and efficacy, and contains divine secrets.*

<summary>This page defines "Philosophers' Gold" not as common gold, but as metals reduced back to their original state of "running Mercury" or quicksilver. Citing Paracelsus, it argues that this substance is the fundamental "root" from which all metals are generated.</summary>
<keywords>Philosophers' Gold, Mercury, Quicksilver, Argent vive, Alchemy, Paracelsus, Spagyric Philosophy, Sulphur of Nature, First Matter</keywords>

## CHAPTER III.

### Of the preparation of Bodies for the Philosophers' Mercury.

**Avicenna** <note>original: "Avicen"; an influential Persian polymath whose medical and philosophical works were foundational to medieval and early modern alchemy.</note> says, "If you desire to operate, you must begin your Work with the solution or sublimation <term>sublimation: the process of heating a substance to a vapor and then cooling it back into a solid to purify it</term> of the two **Luminaries** <note>original: "Luminaries"; in alchemy, the Sun represents Gold and the Moon represents Silver.</note>. This is because the first stage of the **Work** is to create **quicksilver** <note>original: "Argent vive"</note> from them. However, because these more perfect bodies are more tightly bound and have a harder coagulation <note>meaning they are more solid and resistant to breaking down</note>, they require preparation and physical calcination <term>calcination: the reduction of a metal to a powder by heat or chemical action</term> before they can be reduced into **Mercury**. This is not as necessary for **Silver**; because of its purity and softness, our **Water** <note>A specialized alchemical solvent or acid.</note> easily acts upon it. This is not the case with **Gold** and other **metals**, which all require **calcination**. Once calcined, our **Water** acts upon them more easily—especially if those metals that are impure are purified <note>original: "depurated"</note> to achieve a similarity of substance."

Regarding the calcination of bodies from the secret work of the Doctor and Bishop of **Trent** <note>A reference to the Bishop of Trent, who was often cited in alchemical literature as an authority on the "Philosophers' Stone."</note> for the **Philosophers' Stone**: **Metals** that are to be dissolved must first be calcined or purged in **Virgin's Milk** <note>original: "Lac virginis"; an alchemical term for various white-colored chemical solutions, often a mixture containing lead or vinegar</note>. **Silver** <note>original: "Luna"</note>, having been made most fine and subtly shaped, must be dissolved in ***aqua-fortis*** <note>original: "Aqua-fortis"; literally "strong water," referring to nitric acid</note> and distilled rainwater in which **sal ammoniac** <note>original: "Sal Armoniack"; ammonium chloride</note> or common salt has been dis- <meta>catchword: "solved"; the text likely continues: "dissolved."</meta>

<summary>This page outlines the necessary initial steps for preparing gold and silver for alchemical transformation, emphasizing the need for 'calcination' to break down their solid structures. It specifically notes that while silver is soft and easily dissolved, gold requires more intense preparation through heating and chemical purification.</summary>
<keywords>Avicenna, Luminaries, Argent vive, Calcination, Silver (Luna), Gold, Lac virginis, Aqua-fortis, Sal Armoniack, Bishop of Trent</keywords>

...dissolved; then it must be precipitated into a very white **calx** <term>calx: a powdery residue left after a metal has been burned or chemically treated</term> and washed in decanted water. This **calx** must then be **edulcorated** <term>edulcorated: the process of washing a solid to remove soluble salts or impurities</term> in hot rain-water so that all the saltiness and corrosiveness <note>original: "acrimony"</note> may be removed. Afterward, it must be dried, and it will become a most pure **calx**.

But **Gold** must be **calcined** in this manner: Make an *amalgam* with gold. This gold must first be purified <note>original: "depurated"</note> by the "Body of the **black Eagle**" <note>original: "black Eagle"; likely a reference to antimony or a specific chemical process used to purify gold.</note> so that it is made beautiful and glorious beyond measure.
